Nothing in that phase is on its path. The shape — one `Job` row left `pending`, never claimed by `run_once` — reads like a leaked worker task from another module claiming it first, i.e. cross-module contamination rather than a wall-clock assumption (`.superpowers/sdd/task-3-report.md` §9.1). If the audit ever runs, this one needs its own look. **4c/defaults-catalog tally (2026-08-25):** one more, and the first in this family that is not a database-clock comparison at all — `tests/test_scheduler_core.py::test_a_failing_job_is_recorded_and_the_scheduler_survives` starts the scheduler at `poll_seconds=0.01`, sleeps `0.1` s, stops it, and asserts `len(calls) > 1` (ten polls' worth of budget for two). All three passed on unmodified reruns and the branch's final full runs were clean; nothing in phase 9b touches `render/pipeline.py`, `db/models.py`, `scheduler/core.py` or any timestamp path (`.superpowers/sdd/task-4-report.md` §6). **Sixteen known instances.** **hardening-sweep-1 cross-reference (2026-08-30):** row 208 and `docs/research/dev-clock-step/README.md` substantially performed the audit this row has been calling overdue — a measured VM clock-step mechanism traced across the `test_facts_gather`, `test_item_facts` and `test_queue` sightings this row already lists, six assertions made immune or irreducibly named — leaving only the sleep-then-count and leaked-task cousins outside that mechanism's reach. **CLOSED 2026-09-07 (the clock window).** Sixteen instances, one mechanism. `_CLAIM_SQL` (`queue/jobs.py:16-35`) matches `run_after <= now()`; `enqueue` commits (`:98`), so `claim` (`:175-179`) evaluates that predicate in a LATER transaction against a second reading of the clock; this host's clock steps backwards 2.705 s every ~30 s. When a step lands in that window nothing is claimed, the handler never runs, and the test fails on whatever the handler should have written — which is why the same *file* kept failing in *different* tests, and why load mattered without load being the cause (load widens the window, it does not create it). Nine of the outstanding sightings are that one window, including this row's own `test_two_workers_claim_distinct_jobs_independently`, `test_job_parks_after_max_attempts` and `test_reclaim_stale_resets_run_after`. **The "leaked worker task from another module" cousin is retired, not deferred:** `tests/test_worker.py::test_unexpected_errors_also_reschedule` failing with `last_error` `None` and the row left `pending` is exactly this window's signature — the handler never ran — and needs no cross-module contamination to explain it. **The `"999"` note in the 8b tally above is corrected:** nothing about job ids collides and xdist is irrelevant. `tests/conftest.py`'s engine fixture TRUNCATEs `RESTART IDENTITY` between tests, so `jobs.id` is 1 in every test in that file, and each xdist worker owns its own database. The haystack was `created_at` (`api/jobs.py:145`), rendered as ISO-8601 with six fractional digits, against a whole-body substring sweep for `"999"` — roughly one run in 250, load-independent and worker-independent. The sentinel is now seven digits, longer than the longest digit run an ISO-8601 timestamp can emit. **The sleep-then-count shape closes too:** the three surviving fixed-sleep assertions in `tests/test_scheduler_core.py` now wait on the event under a sixty-second ceiling, matching the idiom that file already used for the fourth. The fix is one idiom — `tests/queue_support.py` back-dates `run_after` by an hour, server-side, wherever a test makes a job due and then claims it — applied across `test_queue.py`, `test_worker.py`, `test_api_actions.py` and `test_scheduler_stale_reclaim_job.py`. Zero production lines. Row 208's guard keeps it from being undone. | S–M — a few tests, plus one leaked-task question | — | — |

| 121 | First-start setup wizard (CLOSED — delivered) | End-of-roadmap (user-placed: after the app is otherwise done). Booting with required env absent enters a setup mode that walks the operator through (a) database details, (b) master password, (c) provider API keys. Design constraints recorded at filing: the app cannot set its own env, so collected values need a persistence target (a writable secrets file the loader falls back to is the likely shape — GitOps/ExternalSecrets deployments never enter setup mode and are unaffected); the wizard is a classic unauthenticated attack surface, so it must exist only while unconfigured, demand the master password as its first act, and exit setup mode atomically **Delivered:** the entrypoint became `python -m autoposter.boot`, which re-derives the mode from the credentials and a readable config document at every boot (no stored marker, and the database itself is never part of that decision) and runs `alembic upgrade head` only once both resolve; `build_setup_app()` is a second FastAPI object serving `/api/setup/*` plus the SPA and 503 everywhere else; persistence is `$AUTOPOSTER_STATE_DIR/secrets.env` (0600, atomic, environment-first, which is what makes the ExternalSecrets exemption structural) and `$AUTOPOSTER_STATE_DIR/autoposter.yaml`; the exit is an `os.execv` of a fresh boot. **Two residues, not yet closed.** (a) The homeops side of this is undone: `kubernetes/apps/media/autoposter/app/helmrelease.yaml` carries no `state:` persistence entry, no `autoposter-state` PVC exists beside it, and no `AUTOPOSTER_STATE_DIR` is in the env block — `deploy/README.md` documents the PVC (RWO, 1Gi) mounted at `/state` and the one env entry, onto the `fsGroup: 568` already set, but nobody has made the change. (b) Step 4's `read_config_document(example_config_path())` (`api/setup.py`) is unguarded: an image whose example config document is unreadable answers that step with a 500 rather than a wizard error, so the image build must always carry the example document. **Second iteration (wizard v2), 2026-09-07, same PR:** the flow became password → this deployment's own URL → database (only when nothing resolves from the environment) → one collapsible per system → finish, with BACK on every step past the first (a client-side `current`/`farthest` machine over the server's existing staging — the server needed no change for back). `public_url` is a new top-level `Config` scalar, not frozen and not in `render_version`'s payload; on a deployment whose document already resolves it is staged, used for the registrations and written nowhere, which the finish page names by key. `POST /api/setup/check` is ONE token-gated endpoint over a ten-entry allowlist with a fixed path per system, a scheme/userinfo guard on the four operator-typed addresses and a five-second ceiling — deliberately no private-IP denylist, since every correct target is private, and the module docstring states the residual (a boolean port scan behind the setup token). Plex signs in by PIN: the code and the auth link are served (a documented row 213 exception — minted by plex.tv, public by design), the poll answers a boolean, and the account token is staged under BOTH `AUTOPOSTER_PLEX_TOKEN` and `AUTOPOSTER_PLEX_ACCOUNT_TOKEN` with no exchange (probe: the owned server's `accessToken` equals the account token; shared servers are filtered out server-side). | 135 | Award-year title collisions not catchable at config load (CLOSED — ruling (a)+(e): left as documented, plus the row's own named offline test) | 8a Task 4 finding, documented in the validator (`config/schema.py:480-489`): the built-in-title-collision check enumerates every builder's titles except the dynamic Oscars year collections ("Oscars Winners 2026"), because doing so needs the ceremony dataset the validator does not fetch. A definition accidentally reusing a live award-year title is not caught until the two definitions fight over the same collection at run time. **Widened by phase 10a-1, deliberately and per dynamic type:** a `builder: dynamic` family's titles are the LIBRARY's — offline enumeration is impossible and online enumeration would put a Plex call inside `_titles_must_not_collide`, which runs on every config write — so the builder declares no `titles()`, the engine falls through to `{definition.title}` (9c decision C6), the placeholder title is reserved although no collection is created under it, and the family's real titles are invisible to the validator, to the leftovers report and to the sweep (`src/autoposter/collections/builders/dynamic.py`'s module docstring says this where the next reader of the builder will see it). The backstop is the same one this row already relies on and it is not new: the reconciler's run-time ownership check refuses a collision with somebody else's collection through `resolve_collision` rather than silently adopting it. That backstop covers a FOREIGN collision only -- a collision between two of THIS service's own definitions is not refused at all, because `resolve_collision` sees its own label on the collection and lets both proceed, so the shared collection's filter fights between the two definitions every pass. So the gap is bigger and the failure mode is unchanged. **Phase 10a-2:** the gap itself is unchanged — a dynamic family's titles are still invisible to `_titles_must_not_collide` — but the run-time consequence is no longer only a filter fight. A family's own delete sweep now enumerates by label, so two definitions whose generated titles collide can turn the collision into a delete candidate: the guard at `engine.py:833-845` protects a collection only while EVERY family whose label it carries ran this pass and rebuilt it, so the symmetric case (both families still building, both labels present) stays guarded, but if only one of the two colliding families has ever labelled the collection — the other never reconciled it, or refused — that family narrowing away from the title is enough on its own to make it a candidate. The guards otherwise hold (the ownership label, a `managed_collections` row and `delete_unconfigured` still apply), and the fix is the same one this row's first sentence names. **One concrete instance, found by the dc-split branch review and filed here because the invisibility is this row's:** `production_network`'s static include list carries the network "DC Universe" (`collections/packs.py:826`, the 2018-2021 streaming service), titled bare by the pack's `<<key_name>>` format (`packs.py:963`), so co-enabling it with `content_dc` on a Show library puts two definitions on one collection titled "DC Universe" — invisible to `_titles_must_not_collide` because the dynamic family declares no `titles()`, and at run time the network family's SMART collection versus `content_dc`'s list one makes `shape_conflict` (`reconcile.py:215`) refuse the loser loudly every pass rather than flap membership; pre-existing (`content_universes` carried the same title beside the same pack since 10b) and, unlike the rest of this row, offline-detectable, because `_NETWORK_INCLUDE` is static — rendering every dynamic pack's static `include` values through its own title format against the co-enabled static title table would catch it with no Plex call, which is this row's first concrete test shape. **Second concrete instance, measured in production 2026-08-30 and now fixed report-side:** the invisibility this row records reached the operator as a false accusation — a Movies pass that had just created, labelled and prefixed 19 genre collections then logged `Movies: 55 prior-tool collection(s) left behind` naming those same 19, because `service.unmanaged_prior_collections` → `_managed_titles` → `definition_titles` takes the smart branch at `engine.py:1183-1184`, `DynamicBuilder` declares no `titles()`, and the report is a pure offline call with no `run_cache` to read `generated_titles` from. The **delete sweep was never wrong** — it reads `generated_titles` through `engine._family_state` (`engine.py:837`), so nothing was deleted; only the report lied. Fixed WITHOUT the online enumeration this row rules out: the report now also treats a candidate as managed when it carries one of this service's own FAMILY labels (`service._family_labelled`, prefix-matched case-folded over `builders/dynamic.py`'s `autoposter-dynamic: `, `facts_family.py`'s `autoposter-facts: ` and `credits_family.py`'s `autoposter-credits: `) — the label is ON the collection, so the report can read what it cannot enumerate, and `_titles_must_not_collide` is untouched, so this row's validator boundary stands exactly as written. The other half of that same log line was config: `adopt_from` held `autoposter`, a case-variant of `ownership_label`, and `collections(label=…)` is case-insensitive server-side, so the candidate query returned all 99 of this service's own collections as a prior tool's — now dropped with one warning by `reconcile.adoptable_labels`. **And the root cause under all of it, also fixed here:** Plex canonicalises label case (measured: ONE tag `Autoposter`, tagID 214239, returned for `autoposter`/`Autoposter`/`AUTOPOSTER` alike) while `has_label`/`prior_tool_label`/`protected_label` compared exactly, so every collection this service had created read as FOREIGN — one 2026-08-30 pass wrote **zero** sort titles and logged 103 ownership conflicts against its own work, and a `protect_labels` entry whose case did not match a stored tag left that collection unprotected. All three readers now fold both sides (`reconcile._folded_labels`, `builders/arr.py:187`'s precedent) and return the tag as the server spells it. **Two residues of that hotfix, left deliberately and recorded here because its own review lives under the gitignored `.superpowers/`:** `FakeSection.collections(label=…)` (`tests/test_collection_leftovers.py:41-44`) still matches case-SENSITIVELY, so the double stands in for a server filter whose case-INSENSITIVITY is the very discovery the fix rests on — no assertion is weakened today (the `label_filters` assertions pin the contract either way), but `test_an_adopt_from_entry_that_is_our_own_label_asks_the_server_nothing`'s docstring describes behaviour its own fixture cannot produce; and `reconcile._folded_labels`/`adoptable_labels` casefold without also `strip()`ping, where the cited precedent (`builders/arr.py:190`, `folded.get(name.strip().casefold())`) does both and `adopt_from`/`protect_labels` are operator-typed in exactly the way that motivated the strip there. Neither is a live failure. **CLOSED (recon `.superpowers/sdd/p-row-135-recon.md`, ruling (a)+(e) -- a default the controller stood behind and the user may still overturn):** left as documented, on the grounds the recon lays out -- every CATCHABLE case is already refused (operator-vs-built-in, `config/schema.py:2880-2886`/`:2867-2878`), the FOREIGN case is refused (`collections/reconcile.py:331-334`), the SHAPE case is refused loudly every pass (`shape_conflict`, `collections/reconcile.py:402-452`), and the report-side false accusation this invisibility actually caused in production (2026-08-30) was already fixed without touching the validator (`service._family_labelled`). The residual stays uncatchable at config load, and stays that way: a `builder: dynamic`/`facts_family`/`credits_family` family's titles are the LIBRARY's, offline enumeration is impossible, and online enumeration would put row 162's sixteen ceremony fetches inside `_titles_must_not_collide`, which runs on every config write -- not affordable, and not built. What DOES land is the row's own first-named concrete test shape: `tests/test_collection_catalog.py::test_every_packs_static_titles_are_checked_against_the_managed_titles` renders every READY dynamic/facts pack's static `include` values (plus its `addons` parent keys) through the pack's own `title_format`, for every library type it serves, and asserts none collides with the full static title table (`_managed_titles`, fed every READY preset at once) -- offline, no Plex call, reusing `_rendered_formats`/`dynamic_titles.render_title`. It went RED on the shipped pair before the allowlist beside it was written: `production_network`'s `include` carries the literal value "DC Universe" (`collections/packs.py:1210`, a defunct 2018-2021 streaming service, Kometa's own list), rendered bare by its `<<key_name>>` format (`packs.py:1345-1351`) into exactly `content_dc`'s static title (`collections/catalog.py:1032`, the DC Studios DCU TMDb list) on a Show library. Not renamed -- `content_dc`'s own description promises an operator who already has the old collection gets it back under the same title, and `production_network`'s `include` list is a verbatim transcription of Kometa's own `defaults/show/network.yml` (renaming either is a live-data migration, recon §7/§12.2) -- instead the collision is DECLARED: `_ALLOWED_STATIC_TITLE_COLLISIONS = {("production_network", "DC Universe")}`, beside the test, one pair, one reason, so a NEW collision still fails red and this one does not. At run time the two definitions build different SHAPES (smart vs list), so `reconcile.shape_conflict` already refuses the loser loudly every pass rather than flapping membership -- this costs a refused pass, never data loss, and nothing about the sweep's four independent guards (ownership label, `managed_collections` row, `delete_unconfigured` off by default, `max_deletes`) changes | S — the row's own named test, test-only; no production code, no new refusal, no migration | parity-only | 95 |

| 138 | Settings editor cannot edit list-of-object config (CLOSED — the definitions-editor phase) | Filed by 8a Task 6 out of `frontend/src/pages/Settings.tsx`'s own comment: the generic config editor renders a list of objects read-only, which blocked UI-side editing of `collections.definitions`. **Closed at its literal scope, and NOT by building a generic array-of-object widget.** `Settings.tsx` is deliberately unchanged — its `Field` dispatch still renders a list of objects read-only, which stays true, because `CollectionDefinition` is the only list-of-object field anybody has asked to edit and a generic widget would have had to recurse into `builder`, `params`, `filters` and `schedule`'s four different shapes for one consumer. **What shipped** is a third verb on the panel that already owns the plumbing: `CustomCollectionsPanel` gains **Edit** beside Create and Remove, and a new `DefinitionEditor.tsx` renders a CURATED subset of the twenty-one fields — `title`, `libraries`, `summary`, `sort`, `sync_mode`, `limit`, `labels`, `label_sync`, `item_label`, `sort_title`, `collection_mode`. The subset's rule is loss-freeness: a field is editable only where its stored value has one representation reachable from a plain widget and one unambiguous unset state, and an unset field is written as its key being ABSENT (the overrides document's own revert), never as `null` and never as an explicit copy of the default. **The law the whole thing rests on** is that an edited entry is `{...storedEntry, ...curatedEdits}` spliced into the stored array at its ordinal — so `schedule`, `filters`, `tmdb_summary`, the three `visible_*` flags, `hub_priority`, `changes_webhook`, `builder` and `params` all ride through a save byte-identical, and the entries either side of the edited one are untouched object references. Both halves are pinned (`test_editing_one_definition_does_not_perturb_its_siblings`, `test_an_edited_definition_keeps_every_field_the_edit_did_not_reach`, and the panel's own whole-list write test). **`libraries` is the one field that does not follow the default-equality rule**, deliberately: `None` means "every library in `collections.libraries`", a moving target rather than a fixed value, so scope gets its own explicit "every configured library" control instead of being inferred from all-boxes-checked — otherwise editing a limit would silently re-scope a definition that named its two libraries by hand. The checkbox roster is the union of the config's names and the entry's own, so a library the config no longer lists survives an edit that never touched scope. **`builder` and `params` are shown, not edited** — a registry key typed by hand names builders that do not exist, and a params dict is shaped by the builder that reads it, so the remove-and-recreate route stays the honest one and the panel's note still says so. **`changes_webhook` renders HOST ONLY** (roadmap row 19's field may embed a token), the discipline `notifications.url` gets. **The hard guard moved into the API.** It was UI-only — a disabled Create button — which one `curl` bypasses, and what the bypass costs is not a validation error but every file-defined collection silently ceasing to be built. `api/routes.py::_definitions_guard` now refuses the FIRST stored `collections.definitions` override while the mounted file still lists definitions, from inside `_validated_generation`, so PUT, preview and apply all carry it. The predicate is the transition, not "the file lists definitions": once an override is stored the file's list is already shadowed, and that is the state the editor lives in — refusing there would brick it. Edit needs no UI guard of its own: provenance is uniform in value (`api/collections_builders.py`), so a listing carrying a file row has no override row to edit. **Row 138's own promise about descriptions is kept**: `CollectionDefinition`'s twenty-one `Field(description=...)` strings, served under the `[]` segment since row 217, are the edit form's hover text — no new plumbing, the panel already fetched `/api/config`. **Two residuals, disclosed rather than papered over.** (1) `GET /api/config` still serves every definition's `changes_webhook` IN FULL — `_REDACTORS` is a dotted-path map and `_read_path` walks dicts only, so no list index reaches inside `collections.definitions`. That predates this phase; redacting it server-side would need a per-entry keep marker, and the keep sentinel refuses to resolve inside a list by design (`_sentinel_occurrences` returns `replaceable=False` for any list). The redaction here is display discipline on the surface this phase added, which is what facts C1.3 asked for — the served-surface gap itself is row 226. (2) **The franchise-cap incident's class is NOT closed by this row** — `max_collections` lives in a preset's `PresetCollection.params` tuple in `collections/packs.py`, not in `collections.definitions` at all, so no editor over definition entries can ever reach it. That is **row 225**. Adjudications C1–C4 in `.superpowers/sdd/p-defedit-facts.md` | M — one form component, one splice helper, one API guard | yes — an operator changes a definition's title, scope, limit or labels without removing and re-creating it | 222 (open — `poster_url` becomes one more field in this form), 225 (filed by this phase), 226 (filed by this phase) |
| 139 | Job row doesn't persist the retry budget `fail()` used (CLOSED — deferred-jobs) | PR #66 review finding: the Jobs page's "waiting for Plex" badge (`job.waiting_for_plex`) is computed by `api/jobs.py` matching `job.last_error` against a fixed string prefix at serialize time (`WAITING_FOR_PLEX_PREFIX`), not by reading back which retry budget `queue/jobs.py::fail()` actually applied to that attempt. A future change to the prefix, or a second reason for the same wider budget, silently desyncs the badge from reality. **CLOSED by the deferred-jobs branch (2026-08-29), by removing the fork rather than persisting it.** `ItemNotFound` no longer takes a wider budget — it takes none: the worker routes it to a new `deferred` job state on an unbounded six-hour horizon (`queue/jobs.py`'s `fail(..., defer_seconds=)`, `DEFER_INTERVAL_SECONDS`), so there is no budget left to read back. `api/jobs.py` now derives `waiting_for_plex` and the new `waiting_reason` from `job.state`, which is what the worker itself wrote, and `WAITING_FOR_PLEX_PREFIX` is deleted. The old heuristic was wrong in both directions and both are now pinned by tests: it missed the two other messages `resolve()` raises (`has no media parts yet`, `is not inside any of the`), and it fired on any *pending* row whose last error merely began that way. No residual: `parse_sonarr`'s `seriesadd` intent resolves through the same `PlexClient.resolve()` and raises the same `ItemNotFound`, so the show/season/episode shapes defer identically to the movie one — verified by reading `intake/arr.py` and `plex/client.py:460-490`, not assumed | S | — | — |

| 142 | Cleanups: dead hash helper, pre-resolution preview counts, hub-failure hash (CLOSED — sweep 4) | 8a final review, bundled: (a) `definition_config_hash` (`config/schema.py:388`) is defined but never called anywhere in `src/autoposter` — production-dead; (b) preview's per-definition `adding`/`removing` counts (`collections/engine.py:362-368`) are computed against the current Plex listing before `reconcile_list_collection`'s ownership resolution runs, so a collision-blocked collection's preview counts read as if the write would apply when it will not; (c) a failed `_apply_hub` (`collections/reconcile.py:284`) still lets `definition_hash` store as current, so a Plex-Pass-required hub failure is never retried until the operator edits the definition again. **closed sweep-4, all three:** (a) `definition_config_hash` deleted with its three tests and its two imports — a tree-wide grep finds it called nowhere in `src/autoposter`, and `engine.py`'s docstring no longer cites it. (b) the preview's adding/removing now ask `reconcile.would_proceed` — the read-only half of `resolve_collision`, added for this — before diffing members, so a collision-blocked collection previews (0,0) and the conflict is reported once by the reconcile step that follows. Cost: one `collection.reload()` GET per previewed title that already exists in Plex, the per-title price the real pass already pays; note that the previewed title is now resolved twice per preview, since `reconcile_list_collection` runs the rule again under the real `dry_run`. **The first shape of (b) was wrong, and the review caught it before merge:** gating on `resolve_collision`'s `ok` consumed a three-way answer as a two-way one, so an adopt-eligible collection — refused only because `dry_run` holds the claim back — previewed 0/0 for a write the pass WOULD perform. That is the same class of wrongness this row set out to fix, pointing the other way, and it landed squarely on the migrating-from-Kometa operator adoption exists for. `would_proceed` is the fix, and it takes no `dry_run` by design: it never writes, so there is nothing for the flag to switch, and a preview that passed `dry_run=False` to `resolve_collision` to get the truthful answer would have claimed ownership of the collection it was only describing. Both directions are pinned — an adopting preview shows the real deltas, and a collection carrying a protected label as well as an adoptable one still previews (0,0) with "protected" reported once, which fixes the check ORDER through the preview path. (c) a failed `_apply_hub` makes `apply_collection_settings` answer not-ok, and all three storing sites then store `""` (the sentinel `api/collections_builders.py` already writes) instead of the definition hash, so the next pass retries. The disclosed cost of retrying at all: a permanently-Pass-less server with `visible_*` set now reports the failure every pass instead of falling silent for ever. The hub write is issued LAST rather than in the position the plan wrote, because `_apply_labels`, `_apply_sort_title` and `_apply_mode` contain no `try`/`except` — issuing the one contained write first would have left a hub write applied on an exception path where today it is not. **Residual, filed as row 215:** (b)'s stated principle is still half-implemented — the preview mirrors the ownership rule but not the shape rule | S — three small, independent fixes | — | — |

| 147 | MDBList error-body caching delays budget recovery (CLOSED — hardening sweep 1) | 8b wrap finding (behaviour already documented in `facts/mdblist.py::list_items`, consequence not yet accepted): MDBList answers a spent daily budget with HTTP 200 and `{"error": "API Limit Reached!"}`, which `fetch_json` stores in the provider cache like any other successful response. The `run_cache` memo stops the *spending* within a pass, but a later pass — including one after midnight, when the allowance has already rolled over — is served the cached refusal and re-raises it until the entry ages out, up to a full TTL late. Either do not cache a limit-error body at all, or add `ProviderCache.delete` and drop the key when the error is recognised. **closed hardening-sweep-1:** the first option, mirroring `tmdb_budget`'s never-cache-a-refusal law — `fetch_json` gained a `cacheable` veto consulted on the decoded body after `raise_for_status()` and before the cache write, and MDBList declines the write for a limit body at both endpoints (`_not_a_limit_body`, which shares `_LIMIT_ERRORS` with the raise sites so the veto and the exception cannot disagree). Tests mirror `test_a_refusal_body_is_never_cached`. **Recovery latency, reasoned:** within a pass the builder memo still stops the spending; across passes the first ask after rollover reaches MDBList, pinned by `test_the_first_ask_after_rollover_reaches_the_transport_again` — so recovery is one request after midnight, not TTL-late. **With one honest exception:** entries written BEFORE this deploy are still served by the read path, and MDBList's default TTL is `24 * 3600`, so for up to 24h after deploy the old behaviour persists on already-poisoned keys. Self-healing, not worth code, but the recovery number is only true after that window. **A NON-limit error body still caches, deliberately** — it is a stable answer (the 404 analogue), and not caching it would spend budget re-asking about dead lists; pinned rather than merely documented by `aad14b2`, which asserts a `{"error": "List not found"}` response DOES write a `provider_cache` row. **Finding, not a to-do:** MDBList is the only provider in this tree that signals a refusal inside a 2xx. Every other client's refusal is a non-2xx and `raise_for_status()` fires before `.json()` and before any write — TVDB's double-401 included, which is structurally immune for the same reason `tmdb_budget` gives for TMDb's 429. A grep of `providers/` for post-2xx error-body inspection found no other site, so `cacheable`'s single caller is the only one that exists, not the first of many. | S | — | — |
| 154 | `added` filter is timezone-dependent on the runner, not the Plex server (CLOSED — sweep 2) | 9a Task 2 fix-round finding (`src/autoposter/collections/filter_values.py`, `filters.py::_as_calendar_date`): Plex sends `addedAt` as a unix epoch, and plexapi's `toDatetime` converts it with `datetime.fromtimestamp(value)` — no `tz`, because plexapi's own `DATETIME_TIMEZONE` is `None` — so the value lands as a naive datetime in the RUNNER's local clock, not the Plex server's, verified directly against the installed plexapi 4.18.2 (`plexapi/utils.py::_parseTimestamp`, `plexapi/video.py:44`). `_as_moment` (renamed from `_as_calendar_date` in Task 4) compares at the MOMENT, so the same item's `added` can fall on either side of an `added.after`/`added.before` boundary depending on which timezone the pass ran in. **9a Task 4 update, and it widens the row:** the comparison was date-granular when this was filed, which confined the effect to items added within a few hours of midnight. Task 4's Kometa oracle moved it to the moment (Kometa compares the plexapi datetime as it stands against a midnight `validate_date` result and against `current_time = datetime.now()`), so a runner-clock offset now shifts EVERY `added` comparison by that offset, not only the ones near midnight — and `added.after`/`added.before` DO ship as of 9a, so the row is exercised. Note also that fixing it is not simply "convert to UTC": the Plex server's own zone is the value that would make `added` mean what an operator reads it as, and the listing does not carry it. Open behavioral question, not yet decided: normalize to a fixed zone before the comparison, or document the runner-dependence and accept it (today's posture). **9b widens it again, in the other direction, and leaves it OPEN.** A `plex_search` date predicate is evaluated by the PLEX SERVER, in the server's clock; a `filters:` date predicate is evaluated by the runner, in the runner's. So one definition can now carry two date predicates that disagree about the same item, and neither is wrong — they are answering in different zones. Not reconciled, deliberately (D6: document the divergence, do not reconcile): the fix is still not "convert to UTC", and forcing either side onto the other's clock would make a collection depend on where the pass ran. What 9b did instead is document it in both places (`src/autoposter/collections/builders/plex_search.py`'s module docstring and `PlexSearchParams`' docstring) and recommend the relative-window forms (`added: 30`, `last_played.not: 6o`) for search dates, which are day-granular or coarser and therefore insensitive to any offset short of a day. **The 9b probe did NOT measure this row, and the number that looks like it did must not be read that way.** Task 5's dual-path round-trip agreed 651/651 on `added.after: 2025-07-25` with zero differing rating keys — but the same run measured the boundary's exposure and found **0 items whose `addedAt` falls within 24h of it** (`docs/research/plex-search-probe/README.md` §4). Only an item inside the server/runner offset of the boundary could ever have disagreed, so that agreement is evidence the predicate could not tell, not evidence the two clocks concur. The measurement this row still wants is a boundary chosen to sit ON a dense part of the `addedAt` distribution, run twice with the runner's `TZ` deliberately offset. **closed sweep-2, on the row's third option:** the doc note IS the answer, per 9b's D6 one module over — any fixed zone would make membership depend on where the pass ran, and the server zone an operator means is not in the listing. The added row's note now states the widened blast radius correctly (every comparison shifts by the offset, not only near-midnight ones) and carries `plex_search`'s relative-window recommendation; `_as_moment` records the adjudication. No behaviour change; the probe this row wanted (a boundary with non-zero exposure) remains unrun and would only inform a conversion this close declines | S — a decision, then either a one-line conversion or leaving the doc note as the answer; the probe that would inform it is a boundary with non-zero exposure | parity-only (exercised since 9a: `added` filters ship; since 9b both sides of the same definition can) | 96 |

Two distinct classes, and they want different answers. (a) **Readable, just not for free** — `genre` (listing truncates to two tags per item), `label` (absent from the listing, present in metadata), `collection` (present for 257/1955 movies and wrong where absent), `audio_language`/`subtitle_language` (the listing stops at `<Part>`; streams never reach it). | 165 | Scheduler head-of-line blocking and the Plex walk's timeout discipline | Filed by the row79/tracearr wrap from two production incidents on 2026-08-25, both of which this branch answered with *visibility* rather than with containment. (1) **The scheduler loop is sequential.** `Scheduler.run` (`src/autoposter/scheduler/core.py:132-143`) awaits `_maybe_run` for each job in turn before it sleeps, so a job that hangs inside `job.run` never returns and every other job's *claim* is never even attempted — no error, no log line, no `scheduled_runs` update, and a dashboard that reads idle rather than stuck. The claim is not a lease either (`claim_due`'s own docstring says so), so the row it holds falls due again while it is still running. The production trigger was a pod whose scheduler stopped claiming anything and stayed that way; **that stall is still unexplained** — the pod was replaced before anything was captured from it and the evidence died with it, so this row starts from the mechanism rather than from a diagnosis. (2) **The Plex walk has no per-request budget of its own.** `PlexServer` is constructed with no `timeout` argument in both `main.py` and `collections/__main__.py`, so every probe falls back to `plexapi.TIMEOUT` (30 s) and the prune's ~15k-row walk carries no overall deadline at all — one wedged socket extends the pass without bound, which is exactly the input the sequential loop above turns into a dead scheduler. What already shipped in response, and why it is not enough: a claim-time `scheduler: %s started` INFO line (`scheduler/core.py:161`), the derived `running` / `interrupted` states on the dashboard's scheduled-job rows (`api/snapshots.py::_run_status`, decided against the process boot instant rather than guessed from a duration), and the boot-time database-vs-process clock delta line. All three make a stuck pass *visible*; none of them bound it. | 166 | Settings editor cannot type a null-valued config field | Filed by the row79/tracearr wrap after an attempted copy-only fix turned out to need the API to change. `Field` (`frontend/src/pages/Settings.tsx:217-288`) picks its widget off the *served* value's runtime type, so a field whose value is `null` has no type to read and falls through to a read-only `(not set)` render; `version_check.harbor_url` was the live example, sitting beside its editable `project`/`repository` siblings. **Decided and delivered by the version-check-from-image-ref task, by deletion rather than by the fix this row asked for:** `harbor_url`/`project`/`repository` turned out not to be an operator preference at all — the registry, project and repository are deployment facts, fully derivable from the image reference the pod already runs, so a config field for them was the wrong shape from the start. They moved out of the schema entirely, into one parsed env var (`AUTOPOSTER_IMAGE_REF`, `src/autoposter/config/image_ref.py`) read once at boot; `version_check:` is no longer a config section, and a YAML that still has one is refused at load rather than silently ignored (`Config._version_check_moved_to_an_env_var`). There is no longer a null-valued field here for the widget to mis-render, and no Settings section to visit. Footnote, not a row of its own: the general question this row raised — a config field whose value is `null` with no schema-carried type for the widget to fall back on — is not known to occur anywhere else in the schema today; it survives only as this sentence unless another field is found to hit it. | 181 | Query folding, deliberately not built | 9b's D2(b), filed so it is not re-litigated by the next reader who notices the redundancy. A `filters:` block on a `plex_search` definition stays CLIENT-side and runs after resolution (`engine.py:457-474`), exactly as it does on any other definition — the server narrows, the client refines, and the overlap between the two is evaluated twice on purpose. Folding the overlapping predicates into the query would be a real optimisation (fewer items resolved, less metadata read) and its correctness surface is why 9b declined it: the two vocabularies are **not the same set** (26 names overlap, 44 are filter-only, 29 search-only — row 96), and the two evaluate dates in **different clocks** (row 154). The yardstick that makes it airtight: bare `es` alone also returns 0, so the zero is the conjunction and not an empty library. `plex_search` reproduces this faithfully and the params docstring tells operators to write language predicates under `any:`. The second half of the finding is about the VOCABULARY: those choice lists are 46 `audioLanguage` and 115 `subtitleLanguage` values spanning 2-letter (`es`), locale tags (`es-419`, `en-GB`), 3-letter (`arc`, `eng`, `fin`, `mul`, `zxx`), script-qualified (`zh-Hans`, `sr-Latn`, `yue-Hant`) and one literal English word, `english` — **no single normalisation target is correct on this library**. Any future language preset must use `any:` and must expect the mix; a preset built on an `all:` base with an expanding code builds empty collections and reports success. **CLOSED by phase 10b, both halves discharged, and neither by the candidate fix this row proposed.** *The conjunction half is the ENGINE's, structurally.* A dynamic family does not choose a base: every bucket's filter is parsed with `base="any"` hard-wired at the one call site (`collections/builders/dynamic.py`'s `parse_filters(..., base="any")`), so a language key expanding to three locale variants is an OR — the 24 and the 442, not the two zeros. Vendoring the ISO table is row 190, which stays open and is names-only. | 187 | A removed `summary:` is never cleared in Plex (CLOSED — sweep 2) | 9c whole-branch review, Minor M-B. `smart.py`'s write path only ever SETS a summary (`if summary is not None`), which is the stance `lists.py:286` already takes (`if summary:`) and is therefore precedent-consistent rather than a 9c divergence. It bites harder on the smart path for one reason: the summary is IN the definition hash, so deleting `summary:` from a definition does change the hash and does trigger a pass — a pass that then performs no summary edit at all, and stores the new hash as current. Fixing either means deciding what clearing a summary means for a field Plex locks once written — the lock is why the current stance exists. **closed sweep-2, decided as CLEAR gated on the lock:** `_clear_collection_summary` (empty value + `summary.locked=0`, the item-level route) fires from both reconcilers' write paths, and only where two separate conditions hold. First, the caller must be able to ASSERT that the definition carries no summary — a licence passed as data and defaulted off, so the two cases that cannot assert it are structurally out of reach: a failed `tmdb_summary` pull resolves to the same `None` an absent summary does and would otherwise clear a healthy summary while reporting "unchanged", and the `dynamic`/`credits` families refuse `summary:` at load, so a summary on one of their collections can only be Plex's or the operator's. Second, the field must carry the lock every managed write leaves; an unlocked summary is never touched. Disclosed consequence, pinned by test: a Plex-UI hand-edit locks too, so on a managed collection with no `summary:` it is cleared by the next pass that writes the collection — for `smart_filter`/CS buckets that means the next definition-changed pass (membership plays no part in `smart_definition_hash`), for the list family (IMDb charts, Oscars, `imdb_list`) it is any pass whose source membership changed, since `lists._members_hash` hashes the resolved members too — the sync-mode stance either way. No consumer of the old `str(error)` was left broken — `LibraryOutcome.error` is read only by `service.py`'s own `ok` and `summary`. The operator-visible change is real and intended: a failed library now reads `Movies: failed (RuntimeError)` on the served surfaces, with the message and traceback one hop away in the pod log. Closed together with row 136, which is the same line. | S — redact at the rollback boundary, plus a test per surface | none (a leak, not a feature) | — |
| 189 | `original_language` and `origin_country` are dynamic types nothing here can enumerate (CLOSED — the prefetch phase, Phase A) | Filed by phase 10a-1's wrap, out of adjudication C3's OUT list, so the two types the engine visibly lacks are a row somebody can read rather than a silence. Both ARE Kometa dynamic types (`meta.py:36-37`) and neither is a library enumeration: upstream builds them as PLAIN collections whose membership comes from a full-library **TMDb walk** — every item's TMDb record fetched, `original_language` / `origin_country` read off it, and the distinct values collected (`meta.py:971-993`, `builder.py:360-374`). So they are not one `listFilterChoices` call short of shipping; they need the metadata-prefetch budget the same rows 155/180 keep running into — a per-item external fetch across the whole library, with its own cache, its own rate limit and its own failure mode when TMDb does not know an item. Plex's own `<Country>` is NOT a substitute: it is the production country as Plex's agent recorded it, which is what the shipped movie-only `country` type already enumerates (row 174), while `origin_country` is TMDb's field and the two disagree on exactly the co-productions an operator would notice. Deliberately not built in 10a-1, and 10a-2 does not want it either — this is 10b/10c company, once someone decides what a whole-library TMDb walk is allowed to cost. **CLOSED by the prefetch phase (Phase A), and the answer to "what is a whole-library TMDb walk allowed to cost" turned out to be nothing.** Every clause of the blocker above is now shipped, taken in this row's own order. **The per-item external fetch: unchanged, and that is the finding.** There is no walk. The three fields ride the `/movie/{id}` and `/tv/{id}` reads the facts pipeline was ALREADY making and discarding — `_countries` and `_language` (`facts/tmdb_facts.py:60`, `:78`) read them off the details payload the parser already had, so the budget bought **zero new requests**. That `origin_country` is present on `/movie/{id}` at all was settled by live capture before anything was planned around it, not assumed: the recon's caution (it was a later TMDb addition, and might have needed `append_to_response` or a second endpoint) did not hold. `_countries` declines a bare string, because `"US"` would otherwise iterate to `["U", "S"]` — a plausible wrong value, which is worse than an absent one. **Its own cache:** `provider_cache`, already there, and a refusal never enters it — `providers/fetch.py` writes the cache *below* `raise_for_status` (`:47` then `:50`), so a 429 body cannot become an answer with a TTL. That is row 147's MDBList defect refused by construction rather than by care, and a mutation moving the write above the raise turns the assertion red. **Its own rate limit:** `tmdb_rate_state` (a single row pinned to `id=1`) and `operations.tmdb_backoff_seconds` (default 60; `0` disables the shared window and is today's behaviour byte for byte). Both sides of every comparison come from the DATABASE clock, so pods behind one database share one window instead of each discovering the 429 for itself — `ImdbMissRefreshState`'s pattern, cited at the site. `Retry-After` is honoured in its delta-seconds form only (parsing the HTTP-date form would mean trusting a *remote* clock to set a window kept on the *database* clock), capped at an hour against a hostile header, and a refusal WIDENS an open window rather than overwriting it. **Its own failure mode when TMDb does not know an item:** `MediaItem.facts_attempted_at` is now stamped on every attempt, unconditionally and above the empty-gather short-circuit (`facts/gather.py:184`) — so "TMDb has nothing for this item" and "nobody has looked" stopped being the same two NULLs, which is exactly the state the enumeration's missing-value rule reads. **What shipped ON that budget:** `builder: facts_family`, with `origin_country` and `original_language` as two of its three types, both library types each. Not two `DYNAMIC_TYPES` rows, and the reason is structural rather than stylistic — it is this row's own first sentence read one layer down: a facts-enumerated type's MEMBERSHIP cannot be a Plex smart filter, because Plex has no `origin_country` field, so a row in that table would be a row its builder cannot build and, worse, one the params validator would advertise to an operator as an option (`src/autoposter/collections/facts_family.py`'s module docstring states this whole argument). What IS shared is everything that matters: `dynamic_keys.derive_keys` and `dynamic_titles.family_titles` are byte-unchanged and have never known where the values came from, so Kometa's addon merges, its `include` whitelist, its `other` leftovers bucket and its duplicate-title refusal are phase 10a's, reused rather than rebuilt. **Coverage is honest rather than complete, and stays that way by design:** the family enumerates what the facts pipeline has VISITED, so a half-swept library builds a half-sized family — correct, incomplete, converging. At the shipped defaults (`scheduler.drift_batch_size` 500 every `scheduler.drift_days` 7) a 16,000-item library is worked through in 32 weeks. **That is arithmetic over two shipped defaults, not a measurement, and no coverage percentage appears anywhere in this roadmap** — the only database this phase could reach was the empty isolated test one, which has no schema at all. What an operator gets instead is per-pass and true: a family that builds nothing reports both numbers ("visited N of M item(s) there") in the pass's own actions | M–L — a budgeted per-item TMDb walk with its own cache, then two type-table rows | parity-only | 102, 155, 180 |
| 190 | A dynamic language family is named by Plex, not by TMDb's ISO table (CLOSED — the location-names phase) | Filed by phase 10a-1's wrap as its own row because it is the phase's one behavioural divergence from upstream that an operator can SEE, and a divergence that lives only in a module docstring is one nobody finds. Upstream titles an `audio_language` / `subtitle_language` bucket from TMDb's `_iso_639_1` table — `en` becomes "English" — and falls back to the Plex choice's own title when TMDb has no entry (`meta.py:928-929`). This service had no such table at filing, so it shipped upstream's fallback branch as its only branch (`src/autoposter/collections/dynamic_types.py`). **Membership is unaffected and provably so:** the KEY is `choice.key` either way, so only the collection's NAME differs, and `title_override` sets it by hand today. On the production libraries the two agree for the common codes (`en=English`, `fi=Finnish`); where they part is the locale variants Plex already titles well — `es-419` is "Spanish (Latin America)" — so it is not obvious the TMDb table is even the better name. Closing this meant vendoring the ISO table (or reading TMDb's `/configuration/languages`), a data dependency for a cosmetic gain, which is why it was filed rather than built. **Which branch phase 10b's two language packs took, recorded here so a reader of this row does not have to go and look:** the Plex-names branch, with the ISO table still unvendored at that time. Neither `media_audio_language` nor `media_subtitle_language` shipped a `title_override` table, and not as a shortcut — there was nothing to transcribe. Upstream's `audio_language.yml`/`subtitle_language.yml` carry no code→name mapping at all (the names come from `meta.py:928`'s run-time `_iso_639_1` lookup), so a transcribed table would have been invented rather than copied, which is exactly what the catalog's provenance rule refuses. What the packs DO carry is upstream's own one-entry `key_name_override` — `myn` → "Mayan", the single non-ISO-639-1 code in the 187 whose TMDb lookup misses — transcribed as the `key_name` the `title_format` then wraps ("Mayan Audio"), not as a finished title. **CLOSED by the location-names phase — half by shipping, half by decision, and the "cosmetic gain not worth a data dependency" reasoning above is superseded rather than merely dated.** The dependency stopped being this row's to justify: row 196 needed the same fetch for a reason that was not cosmetic at all (two packs that could not ship), so the table arrived as that row's cost and this one names it for free. It exists now — TMDb's `/configuration/languages` `english_name` table, fetched once and vendored at `collections/iso_names.py` as `LANGUAGE_NAMES`. The family that had NO title source at all uses it: `original_language` buckets are titled through the table with the code-as-title fallback for any code it misses, keys untouched (row 156's law). One wrinkle was found by measuring the table rather than trusting it: TMDb gives both `nr` and `nd` the name "Ndebele", and because this family keys on the CODE, two keys would claim one title and `DuplicateFamilyTitle` would refuse EVERY language collection, not just the clashing pair. `language_title` appends the code to any name more than one code carries — "Ndebele (nr)", "Ndebele (nd)" — a suffix that is OURS but invents no vocabulary, being the family key every narrowing knob already speaks. **The two shipped Plex-enumerated families deliberately KEEP Plex's own titles**, and that is a decision this row's own evidence argues for, not a shortfall: this row observed that Plex titles the locale variants better — `es-419` is "Spanish (Latin America)", which a bare ISO-639-1 table cannot say — so renaming two shipped families onto a worse-informed source is refused. The table is one import away if that judgement ever changes, and both pack descriptions still say who names their buckets | S — vendor or fetch the ISO-639-1 name table, then one lookup with the existing fallback | parity-only (names only, never membership) | 102, 196 (the fetch that supplied the table) |
