| 193 | `tests/test_worker.py` fails intermittently on full-suite runs — four different tests, six sightings (CLOSED — the same clock window) | Filed by phase 10b's wrap because the FILE has now been seen **four times across two phases** as of 10b's wrap, and a fifth time since, on the deferred-jobs branch (see the dated note at the end of this row) — two 10a-2-era sightings (its Task 1 dispatch run, and its whole-branch review, raised there as a ⚠️ and not as a failure) and two of 10b's own, an hour apart on 2026-08-27: Task 4's full-suite run (`.superpowers/run-t4-full1.log`, 22:22:17 — `test_db_error_in_handler_reschedules_instead_of_stranding_at_running` fails with `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'lower'` on `job.last_error`) and Task 5's full-suite run (`.superpowers/run-t5-full.log`, 23:23:08 — `test_a_cancelled_job_that_succeeds_still_completes` fails with `assert job.state == "done"` getting `pending`) — and a fourth sighting inside one phase is where "someone's flaky machine" stops being the honest reading. **Two different tests, not one repeating shape.** The row was originally filed on the T5 assertion alone; T4's is a different test failing a different assertion, not a repeat of it — but it is the SAME class of defect, a `run_once` write the test's session cannot see (there, the state transition; here, the error bookkeeping written alongside a reschedule), and it is, if anything STRONGER evidence for this row's own transaction-visibility hypothesis: the invisible-write shape recurs across two independent paths through `run_once`, not one assertion misbehaving twice. **What the isolation evidence rules out**, attributed to the sighting each re-run actually followed: it is not a regression from any of this work (`tests/test_worker.py` is untouched by the 10a-1, 10a-2 and 10b branches, appearing in none of their test-file diffs, and the worker is orthogonal to the collections subsystem all three phases changed); it is not deterministic (the file alone re-ran green immediately after the **T4** sighting — `.superpowers/run-t4-worker.log`, 20 passed, 22:22:46 — and the FULL suite re-ran green end to end after the **T5** sighting — `.superpowers/run-t5-full2.log`, 3702 passed 0 failed, 23:34:48 — each with no code change between its own failure and its own green re-run; no file-alone re-run after the T5 sighting is evidenced on disk); and it is not the shared-database collision this project has hit before, since `tests/conftest.py`'s `_database_for_this_process` gives every xdist worker its own database (`<base>_gw0`, `_gw1`, …), so no second worker can be claiming this job. What that leaves — unproven, and named as a hypothesis rather than a diagnosis — is a timing or transaction-visibility race between a test's setup writes and `run_once`'s claim, of the kind a loaded container surfaces and a quiet one does not. **Provenance, stated honestly.** The two 10b sightings each have a durable full-suite log on disk (`run-t4-full1.log`, `run-t5-full.log`, both cited above). The two 10a-2-era sightings have no durable artifact in the repo; they rest on the session ledger's contemporaneous record, `.superpowers/sdd/progress.md`, as their sole provenance. **Why it is a row and not a fix:** every sighting so far has been noticed by a phase whose gate was "full suite green", re-run once, and passed; nobody has yet reproduced it on demand, and a speculative `await`-and-retry in the worker would make the tests pass without anyone knowing whether the production claim path shares the race. The work is to reproduce first — loop **the file**, not the one test, under the suite's own concurrency until it fails, capture the job row's timestamps for whichever test trips — and only then decide whether the defect is in a test's setup or in `run_once`'s claim path. **Fifth sighting, 2026-08-29** (deferred-jobs fix round): a third distinct test, `test_a_cancelled_job_waiting_for_plex_is_also_dismissed`, failed on a full-suite run — one-off, with the file re-run alone coming back 22/22 and a clean full-suite rerun following it, both with no code change in between. Recorded on the session ledger (`.superpowers/sdd/progress.md`), same as the two 10a-2-era sightings, with no durable full-suite log of its own beyond that entry. **Sixth sighting, 2026-08-29** (the prefetch phase, Phase A — Task 2's full-suite run): a FOURTH distinct test, `test_cancelled_mid_db_operation_still_releases_and_propagates`, failed with `Failed: DID NOT RAISE CancelledError` (`test_worker.py:150`) in a run of 1 failed / 3761 passed. Re-run alone with no code change: 22 passed. It has a **durable full-suite log** — `.superpowers/run-ppf-t2-full.log` — which the fifth and the two 10a-2-era sightings do not, and it is unrelated to the work that saw it (the prefetch phase touched the facts pipeline, the TMDb client and the collections engine; the worker is orthogonal to all three, and `tests/test_worker.py` is untouched by the branch). One sighting across this branch's full-suite runs; every other one came back clean. **A methodological finding that belongs in this row, because it bears on the sighting COUNT rather than on any one sighting.** The container command that produced that log is `pytest -q | tee …`, so `docker wait` returned **0 despite the failure** — the exit code was tee's. Any earlier phase that gated a full-suite run on the container's exit status rather than on reading the log file could have passed over a sighting without seeing it, which means the six recorded here are a floor and not a census. Reading the log rather than trusting the wait is now the phase discipline, and it is how this sighting was caught at all **CLOSED 2026-09-07, and the row's premise changes.** The row asks to reproduce first and then decide whether the defect is in a test's setup or in `run_once`'s claim path. The answer is **both, and neither is a bug**: the test setup is at fault, and the claim path's exposure is real production semantics that no test-level change should touch. The row's own hypothesis — "a timing or transaction-visibility race between a test's setup writes and `run_once`'s claim" — is half right in the half that matters: it is cross-transaction, but what crosses is not visibility (the writes are committed and perfectly visible), it is the **clock**. `_CLAIM_SQL` matches `run_after <= now()` in a transaction later than `enqueue`'s commit, and this host's clock steps backwards 2.705 s every ~30 s. The mechanism predicts all four distinct sightings exactly: `state == "pending"` (handler never ran), `AttributeError` on `last_error.lower()` (`fail()` never ran, `last_error` still `None`), `DID NOT RAISE CancelledError` (the raising handler never invoked), and the un-dismissed cancelled job. **Reproduced on demand, not by looping.** A backwards step of N seconds and a `run_after` stamped N seconds ahead are the same arithmetic, so `tests/test_queue.py::test_a_job_stamped_a_step_into_the_future_is_not_claimed` injects the step instead of waiting for a 0.019 %-per-execution event: deterministic, machine-independent, milliseconds. The fix is `tests/queue_support.py`'s hour of back-dating at every site where a test makes a job due and then claims it. `tests/test_worker.py` holds 24 of the converted sites. | S — reproduce under a loop, then one fix in either a test's setup or the claim path | parity-only (test infrastructure; no shipped behaviour known to be affected) | — |
| 194 | The appearance-threshold dynamic person types, and the library-wide credit scan they need (CLOSED — phase B) | Filed by phase 10c-lite, out of adjudication C1, because row 83 SPLIT three ways: its static half (the five filmography builders) shipped in 8c, its bio/poster half shipped in 10c-lite, and this — the expensive half — never got the argument row 192 got for `tmdb_collection`. **What upstream's packs actually are.** All four (`defaults/both/actor.yml`, `defaults/movie/director.yml`, `defaults/movie/writer.yml`, `defaults/movie/producer.yml`) are `dynamic_collections` with `data: {depth: 5, limit: 25}` — one collection per person with at least five appearances in the library, capped at the twenty-five with the most — and **the packs name no people at all** (all four transcribed whole in `.superpowers/sdd/p10c-upstream-person.md` §2.1; the same reading is written above `_STARTER_DIRECTORS` in `src/autoposter/collections/catalog.py`). That is why there is nothing to transcribe into a preset, and why `people_directors` ships six directors that are openly OURS and marked `NOT_KOMETA` in their own row — the only thing borrowed from upstream there is the `<name> (Director)` title shape. **Blocker one — row 169, and it is STRUCTURAL rather than merely missing.** `actor`, `director`, `writer` and `producer` are not rows of `FILTER_ATTRIBUTES`, so `filters.BY_NAME` (`src/autoposter/collections/filters.py:890`, built from that table) has no entry for any of them and the per-person query each of these collections would carry cannot be *written* at all today — not untested, inexpressible. And row 169 needs more than four table rows: Kometa's person resolution runs through `get_actor_id` (`builder.py:4426`), verified by the T1 review (`p10c-task-1-review.md:143-149`) to BE `hubSearch` filtered to `Role` (`plex.py:1273-1280`, the same citation row 169 already carries) — the same Plex person-tag machinery, not a conditional fallback — and it runs only AFTER `get_search_choices` at `builder.py:4412` has already been called and can already have raised. `builder.py:4400-4412` enters the tag branch and `builder.py:4412` → `plex.py:1300-1319` enumerates every actor tag in the library, raising when the library cannot produce the listing. **Blocker two — the credit scan, which is the rows 155/180 budget family.** NAMING the people means reading every credit of every item in the library: a Plex-side per-item people read, through a cache that does not exist yet. Whether that cache is `item_facts` or a table of its own is a DECISION this row records as a decision — made at design time, not discovered at review. **The counts problem, which no choices listing can answer.** `depth` is a count of appearances, and `listFilterChoices` returns VALUES, never counts — so even a library whose cast were enumerable would still not answer the question the pack asks. This is what distinguishes this row from rows 189 and 192, where the enumeration alone was the blocker. **The fan-out, against the number the 10a probe actually measured.** The worst measured enumeration is `studio` at **824** values (`docs/research/plex-dynamic-probe/README.md`), and a library's distinct cast is larger than its distinct studios by an order of magnitude — so this family meets `params.max_collections`' refusal (default 50, which creates nothing and reports both numbers) rather than a truncation. A cap that truncated would be worse than the refusal: it would build a plausible, arbitrary fraction of the family and look finished. **An open question whoever closes this row has to answer explicitly, because nobody has:** upstream's per-person collection is a search on the Plex ACTOR TAG — the library's own credit data — while a `tmdb_actor` filmography is TMDb's credit data intersected with the library. They are different memberships under the same name, and which one this service should build is a decision, not a detail. **`tmdb_popular_people` files here too, on the same evidence.** 10c-lite's decision gate returned VERDICT B-GATED positively rather than as a tie-break: upstream's `tmdb_popular_people` is a dynamic collection TYPE with no builder and no shipped pack (`popular_people` has zero matches in `modules/builder.py`; no `defaults/` file uses the type), and its default template is `meta.py:42` — `{"tmdb_person": "<<value>>", "plex_search": {"all": {"actor": "tmdb"}}}` — so each collection is one person's, given a biography and a photo by `tmdb_person` and its MEMBERS by a Plex actor-tag search. That search is the row-169 machinery again: `actor` is a Plex tag attribute (`plex.py:527`), the literal string `tmdb` is a placeholder only `tmdb_person`'s resolved NAMES can fill (`builder.py:4401-4408`, fed by `builder.py:1409`), and those names are then matched against the library's own actor-tag listing (`plex.py:1300-1319`). Four tag-free candidates were hunted and closed rather than assumed away: TMDb's `known_for` is discarded before Kometa can use it (`tmdb.py:505` keeps only `p.id` and `p.name`), the four shipped packs reach the identical tag branch by a second route through `templates.yml:238-255`'s `smart_filter` template, `meta.py`'s type lists offer no alternative shape, and `defaults/` ships no pack for the type at all. What could ship without the tag and deliberately does not: an expansion into per-person `tmdb_actor` filmographies is buildable TODAY and would be a DIFFERENT membership from upstream's — upstream's collection holds the library items TAGGED with that person, ours would hold the library items TMDb CREDITS them on — so shipping it under upstream's name would be the fabricated attribution this project's provenance rule forbids. If it ever ships, it ships as ours, `NOT_KOMETA`, with that difference stated in the row. Filing it costs no preset parity, because Kometa ships no default pack that uses it. **What 10c-lite DID ship, so this row is not misread as "people do not work":** the five filmography builders (8c) now carry the person's TMDb biography as their collection summary and their TMDb profile photo as its poster (`TmdbListClient.person_detail`, `posters.tmdb_profile_url`), every existing override still wins over both, and `people_directors` is a working preset today. Row 83 CLOSED with it. **Re-pointed by the prefetch phase's wrap.** The prefetch phase split the "metadata-prefetch budget" this row's dependency cell shared with 155/180 into its two real halves and shipped only the TMDb-side one (rows 189/192). That half buys this row nothing: the credit scan this row needs is Plex-side, it needs COUNTS across the whole library rather than a scalar per item, and the recon that scoped the phase named this row as the one consumer of the four that does not share the payload the other three ride on. The budgeted per-item credit scan and the "is the cache `item_facts` or a table of its own" decision are row 197's, taken there as a decision rather than discovered later **CLOSED — phase B, and the whole chain shipped rather than a part of it.** The scan is `collections/credits.scan_library_credits`, which walks the library's owned keys through the same batched `/library/metadata/{k1,k2,…}` read row 197 built (`plex/client.fetch_credit_index`, chunks of 200) and writes `item_credits` rows; it runs on its own `credits_scan` scheduler job at `scheduler.credits_scan_days: 7`, so the cost is a weekly library walk rather than anything a collections pass pays. The counted enumeration is `credits.enumerate_credits`, `facts_enumeration`'s own `GROUP BY` shape returning `(value, count)` — which is the thing `listFilterChoices` structurally cannot answer and the reason this row could not be a dynamic type. The family is a FOURTH builder shape, `builder: credits_family` (`collections/builders/credits_family.py`), one smart collection per person over row 169's four search attributes, taking upstream's own `data:` values as params (`depth: 5`, `limit: 25`). The four Top-* packs flip **READY**: `people_top_actors`, `people_top_directors`, `people_top_writers`, `people_top_producers` (`CATALOG_CHECKSUM["people"]` moves (1,4,0) → (5,0,0)). **The open question this row carried is answered, and the answer is disclosed rather than assumed:** the person key is the **Plex tag name**, because the feed is Plex's own library credit data — the phase-B probe's D1 gate measured Role/Director/Writer/Producer riding the batch (8815 roles across 200 movies) and identical to a single-key fetch on 6 of 6 sampled items, so no TMDb fallback was needed and no filmography-vs-tag divergence arises. **What the packs must NOT claim, and their descriptions say so:** the counts are a FLOOR, not a census. Plex caps `<Role>` at 200 children per item server-side, unescapable by single-key reads, so a person whose every appearance is in a >200-role cast can be absent from the ranking entirely — the ranking is biased against people who only ever appear in large casts. Shows carry **no** director/writer/producer at all (0 in the batch, 0 in a single-key fetch, 0 enumerable) — a property of series-level Plex metadata, those credits living on episodes, which is why the three crew kinds are Movie-only by name. `tmdb_popular_people` stays filed: nothing here changed its verdict, since a popularity chart is not a library enumeration | L — a budgeted per-item credit scan with its own cache, then row 169's person lookup, then one dynamic-type row | parity-only | 169, 197, 102 (engine, closed) |
| 195 | `tests/test_pipeline.py::test_second_upsert_of_the_same_rating_key_refreshes_updated_at` fails intermittently on a full-suite run — one sighting, with a mechanism note (CLOSED — hardening sweep 1) | Filed by phase 10c-lite's wrap as its own row rather than as a sixth sighting widening row 193, because nothing about it is row 193's: a different file, a different subsystem (the render pipeline's upsert, not the worker's claim path) and a different mechanism. **The sighting.** 2026-08-29, phase 10c-lite Task 2's first full-suite run (`.superpowers/run-p10c-t2-full.log`, `1 failed, 3731 passed in 566.63s`). The test writes one `rating_key` twice, `asyncio.sleep(1.1)` apart, and asserts `second.updated_at > first.updated_at`; it got `12:25:31.191867` for the SECOND write against `12:25:32.114473` for the first — the timestamps went BACKWARDS by ~0.92s across two commits separated by a 1.1-second sleep. Not deterministic and not a regression from that phase's work (it touches nothing in `render/`): the file alone re-ran 42/42 immediately after (`.superpowers/run-p10c-t2-pipeline-recheck.log`) and the full suite re-ran `3732 passed` end to end with no code change in between (`.superpowers/run-p10c-t2-full2.log`). Both logs are on disk, which is more provenance than three of row 193's five sightings have. **The mechanism note, which is this row's whole value.** PostgreSQL's `now()` is `transaction_timestamp()` — the moment the enclosing TRANSACTION began, not the moment the statement ran — and both writes take their value from it: the insert path from `MediaItem.updated_at`'s `server_default=func.now()` (`src/autoposter/db/models.py:66-68`) and the conflict path from `_upsert_media_item`'s explicit `set_={..., "updated_at": func.now()}` (`src/autoposter/render/pipeline.py:311`), which exists because SQLAlchemy's `onupdate=` hook never fires on an `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`. Under those semantics the sleep guarantees nothing the assertion needs: what is being compared is the distance between two transaction STARTS, and a `now()` read on a pooled connection whose transaction began earlier returns a timestamp older than one issued before it. That is the only shape that produces the observed INVERSION rather than merely a narrowed gap — and it is a hypothesis with the numbers behind it, not a diagnosis; nobody has reproduced it on demand. **Why it is a row and not a fix**, the same argument row 193 makes: the tempting change (relax to `>=`, or lengthen the sleep) makes the test pass without anyone learning whether the production upsert's `updated_at` is trustworthy, and that column is read outside this test. The work is to reproduce first — loop the FILE under the suite's own concurrency, logging `txid_current()`, `transaction_timestamp()` and `clock_timestamp()` for both writes — and only then decide whether the defect is the assertion's premise or the column's semantics. Cross-reference row 193 as the same observational CLASS (full-suite-only, one-off, green on both the file-alone and the clean full re-run) and nothing more; row 193 itself gains nothing from this phase, whose runs produced no `tests/test_worker.py` sighting at all. **closed hardening-sweep-1: REPRODUCED, and the defect was the assertion's premise, not the column.** A 200-iteration loop of the file under `-n auto --dist loadgroup` (the sighting's own concurrency) failed on **iteration 1** (`.superpowers/run-p-hard1-loop195.log`). The three-clock diagnostic shows BOTH clocks inverted between the two writes — `transaction_timestamp()` **-1.581795 s**, `clock_timestamp()` **-1.570837 s** across a 1.1 s sleep — with `txid_current()` advancing normally (901 → 915, so no transaction reordering) and write1's own `clock` reading 1.8 ms EARLIER than its own `xact`, which is impossible under a monotonic clock and is an independent proof on its own. An independent 120 s probe of `clock_timestamp()` and the container's `time.time()` then measured the cause directly: the VM wall clock that sources both steps backwards by a composite **~2.705 s roughly every ~30 s of real time**, both clocks in lockstep to under 100 µs (the probe's raw 27.28 s cadence is APPARENT time, short by exactly one step per cycle). **The rate, stated correctly:** the bad window is the ~1.12 s interval between the test's two timestamp readings, not the step's magnitude — so exposure is `interval / 30 s` ≈ **3.7 % per execution**, and iteration 1 hitting it was an early draw, not what the rate predicts. Two independent corroborations close on the same step: -1.5818 + 2.705 = 1.1232 s real elapsed (the 1.1 s sleep plus ~23 ms of overhead), and the 2026-08-29 sighting's -0.92 s implies 1.785 s — the same step across an interval stretched wider by full-suite load, which is why the flake looked full-suite-only. Suite length cannot change a test's per-execution probability; it can and does widen the test's own inter-write interval. `clock_timestamp()` steps with it, so switching the column to it would not have helped. **Fixed in `9503df0`:** `assert second.updated_at != first.updated_at`. Mutation-proven still to catch the regression this test exists for — deleting the `set_` re-stamp yields byte-identical timestamps — and 30/30 loop iterations green afterwards (under the old `>` the chance of at least one failure over 30 iterations was ~68 %, not the ~96 % first reported). **The residual, named rather than denied:** `!=` cannot catch a re-stamp that writes an EARLIER value, which is precisely the alternative hypothesis (a `func.now()` serving a transaction start older than the row's existing value). On a machine with a sane clock `>` would catch that class; here it cannot distinguish that defect from the environment, so the relaxation is the minimum the reproduced mechanism licenses and the loss is recorded instead of claimed away. **Row 193: no opportunity to sight.** The loop ran `tests/test_pipeline.py` alone, so `tests/test_worker.py` could not have appeared — this is a null result by construction, not a health signal for row 193, and row 193's code was not touched. **What outlives this row:** `docs/research/dev-clock-step/README.md` (measurement, corrected derivation, reproducer, and the enforceable rule) and row 208, which carries the rule and the six instances. Logs: `.superpowers/run-p-hard1-loop195.log`, `.superpowers/p-hard1-195-diag.txt`, `.superpowers/run-p-hard1-t3-clockprobe.log`. | S — reproduce under a loop, then either correct the assertion's premise or fix `updated_at`'s semantics | parity-only (test infrastructure; whether shipped behaviour is affected is exactly the open question) | 193 (same class, different file) |
| 196 | The region and continent packs group country NAMES; `origin_country` carries ISO codes, and no code→name table exists (CLOSED — the location-names phase) | Filed by the prefetch phase (Phase A) out of adjudication C8's re-file valve, because the two location packs `content`/`location` had been gated on row 189 and row 189 closed **without being able to help them**. Phase A shipped the `origin_country` enumeration and the `facts_family` builder that consumes it, so the machinery those packs were waiting on is done — and they still cannot ship, for a reason nobody had written down until the pack files were fetched whole. **The evidence, measured rather than recalled.** `defaults/movie/region.yml` and `defaults/movie/continent.yml` both declare `type: country` with `search_term: {default: country}` — they group **Plex's own `country` values, which are display names**. Their grouping tables carry **647 member strings between them and zero ISO codes** (23 `include` entries and 22 addon keys over 323 members for region; 6 and 5 over 324 for continent), and the tables deliberately carry alias SPELLINGS of the same country — `Türkiye` beside `Turkey`, `Soviet Union` beside `Russia`, five spellings of the US Minor Outlying Islands — precisely because they are matching free text a Plex agent emitted. A table of ISO codes would need none of that. TMDb's `origin_country`, the field this service now stores, is an array of **ISO-3166-1 alpha-2 codes**, confirmed by live capture and not assumed: the captured `/movie/{id}` and `/tv/{id}` payloads both read `origin_country: ["US"]` while the same payloads' `production_countries` spell the same country out as `{"iso_3166_1": "US", "name": "United States of America"}`. **The two do not join without a code→name table, and at filing nothing supplied one:** not the fetched pack files (no country data at all in `franchise.yml`; names only in the other two), and not this tree — the same absence row 190 recorded for languages. **So a flip would not have been a transcription.** It would have been upstream's grouping tables plus a mapping layer this service invented, which is exactly the silent substitution row 189 rules out when it is not declared, and exactly what `collections/catalog.py`'s provenance rule refuses. That is the gap the close below fills: the table is no longer invented, it is fetched bytes with provenance, so the flip IS a transcription plus a lookup through a stamped table. The packs therefore stayed GATED at the time of filing and re-pointed here rather than shipping an approximation under Kometa's name — the 10b pack discipline, "a pack that can't ship honestly re-files". **What closing this row took**, and it was a decision before it was any code: vendor (or fetch, e.g. TMDb's `/configuration/countries`) an ISO-3166-1 alpha-2 → name table, then decide which SIDE is normalised — map our codes up into upstream's display names so its 647 strings keep working, or rewrite both grouping tables into codes, which is a re-transcription of two files rather than a lookup. The `location_country` pack beside these two was unaffected and shipped throughout: it enumerates Plex's own `country` tag, so its keys are already the display names upstream's tables were written against. It is also why the two packs' descriptions have to say they are a DIFFERENT value set from that one — an operator can enable all three, and they disagree on exactly the co-productions they would notice. **CLOSED by the location-names phase, and the gate above is now answered rather than open.** The decision came first, as this row asked. The table is FETCHED — `/configuration/countries` (and `/configuration/languages` beside it), once, provenance-stamped, vendored by script as `collections/iso_names.py` — and the side normalised is OURS: `origin_country`'s codes map UP into TMDb's English names, so upstream's 647 member strings work verbatim and both grouping tables are transcribed byte-for-byte (`packs.py`, digest-guarded at two sites). The join was MEASURED per-name before either pack flipped (`docs/research/tmdb-iso-names/README.md`). The library-side join the plan's gate named turned out to have an empty denominator — production's `tmdb_origin_country` is `[]` on all 13590 rows because the drift sweep has not revisited anything since Phase A merged — so the controller re-scoped the gate to the FORWARD join, which answers the same semantic question from the half of the evidence that exists: **241 of 251** fetched TMDb country names (96.0%) land in `region.yml`'s member universe and the same **241/251** in `continent.yml`'s, 243/251 (96.8%) accent-folded, with the two miss lists identical name for name. All ten misses are spelling divergences for countries upstream does carry — `Faeroe Islands`/`Faroe Islands`, `Kyrgyz Republic`/`Kyrgyzstan`, and eight more — and **not one of the 251 is absent as a place**, so a ten-entry OURS-marked `COUNTRY_NAME_ALIASES` table bridges them, each pair derived by comparing the two fetched artifacts on disk rather than recalled. Anything still unmatched falls into `Other Regions`/`Other Continents` honestly — upstream's own leftovers rule. `location_region` and `location_continent` ship READY; the `location_country` pack beside them is untouched; all three descriptions disclose the shared titles, and the two new packs' descriptions state that the value sets disagree on co-productions | M — a vendored or fetched country-name table, one normalisation decision, then two pack transcriptions | parity-only (two packs that do not ship) | 189 (closed), 190 (the same absence for languages), 102 |
| 197 | The Plex-side batched metadata read, the credits cache, and where that cache lives (CLOSED — phase B) | Filed by the prefetch phase (Phase A) out of adjudication C1, which SPLIT something this table had been calling one thing. **What "the metadata-prefetch budget" turned out to be: two unrelated mechanisms wearing one name.** Rows 91, 96, 189 and 192 all carried the phrase — row 155 was named as its home by the others rather than being a carrier of it — and the recon that scoped Phase A found three of those consumers (189, 192, and the location packs) share ONE TMDb payload the facts pipeline was already fetching and discarding — a scalar or two per item, rideable for free. The remaining consumers are Plex-side, want a different shape of answer, and share nothing with that payload: row 155's six stranded attributes want per-item metadata Plex's section listing truncates or omits, row 91 wants `item.collections` per item, and row 194's people scan wants library-wide COUNTS. Phase A shipped the TMDb half and closed 189/192 on it. **This row is the other half, and it is a phase rather than a row's worth of work.** Three parts. **(1) The batched read.** `/library/metadata/{k1},{k2},{k3}` — the path-segment form; the query-parameter spelling that stood in row 155 until Phase A's wrap corrected it does not exist in vendored plexapi 4.18.2. Its cost is measured rather than guessed: **10 calls / 16.90s for all 1955 movies at chunk=200** (`.superpowers/sdd/archive/p9a-task-2-report.md` §2, phase 9a's own read-only probe — no probe in Phase A spoke to Plex at all). What that buys is the (a) half of row 155: `genre`, `label`, `collection`, `audio_language`, `subtitle_language`. It does not buy `network`, which Plex 1.43.4 emits nowhere and no budget can conjure — that half of 155 is a decision, not a fetch. **(2) The credits cache**, which is row 194's scan and, as a neighbour rather than a blocker, what row 169's four people attributes would read from. **(3) The decision this row exists to make BEFORE it is discovered: is that cache `item_facts`, or a table of its own?** Phase A deliberately did not prejudge it (adjudication C3 says so in as many words) and could not have: the three fields it added are scalars and fit a row on `item_facts` uncontroversially, while credits are many-to-many — one item has many people, one person is in many items — and a scalar table is the wrong shape for that whether or not it can be made to hold it. Deciding it late means either a migration that moves data or a second cache that quietly disagrees with the first, which is why it is written here as the first thing this phase does. **What this row does NOT inherit from Phase A:** the budget itself, because Plex is local and the cost being managed is reloads rather than a remote rate limit. What it CAN reuse in shape if it ever needs to is the pattern Phase A proved: a shared window on the database clock rather than a process variable, a refusal that never enters the cache, and an attempt stamped whether or not it found anything. **Not started, and deliberately its own recon-to-plan cycle** — Phase A's scope decision was that a phase which had already shipped a widening, a rate budget, an enumeration seam, a third family shape and a pack flip should stop rather than take on a second subsystem **CLOSED — phase B, which was that recon-to-plan cycle.** The batched read is `plex/client.fetch_tag_index` over `_iter_metadata_batches`, which hands plexapi's own list-of-ints `fetchItems` (it already translates one to `/library/metadata/{k1,k2,…}`, base.py:334-335 — the seam existed unused) in chunks of `TAG_BATCH_CHUNK = 200`, deduplicating keys first; the per-run enrichment cache is `collections/enrichment.ensure_tags`, memoised on the pass-level `run_cache` and scoped to the RESOLVED ITEM SET of the definitions that need it, never the whole library, with `test_call_count_is_ceil_n_over_chunk_exactly` pinning `ceil(1955/200) == 10` calls exactly. The chunk size needed no reduction: the phase-B probe measured 1200 keys / 7671 path characters working and ~1600 refused as a plexapi `BadRequest` carrying no status code, but found that COST binds long before the cap does — 8–12 ms/item at chunks of 50-200 against 15.5/25.2/36.7 at 400/800/1200 (`docs/research/plex-batch-probe/README.md`, probe c). **The cache-shape decision was taken as this row exists to take it, and it went to a DEDICATED table:** `item_credits`, composite PK `(item_id, kind, person)` with a CASCADE FK to `media_items` and an attempt stamp on the ITEM (`credits_attempted_at`), migration `a4db89c94eaa` — `item_facts` is scalar-per-item with a `UNIQUE(item_id)` and credits are many-to-many, so it was the wrong shape and answering that here rather than discovering it later is the whole reason this row was filed. Refusals are never cached; no row means ABSENT from every query, never a zero. The scan is `collections/credits.scan_library_credits` on a `credits_scan` scheduler job at `scheduler.credits_scan_days: 7`. **What the batched read may not claim:** Plex caps `<Role>` children at 200 per item server-side and a single-key fetch does not escape it (probe b: 54 of 200 sampled shows land on exactly 200), so anything counting credits reports a floor | L — its own phase: a recon, the cache-shape decision, the batched read with a reload budget, then the rows that unblock | parity-only (nothing ships today; six rows wait on it) | 155, 91, 194, 169 (neighbour), 96, 180 (destination for one branch of its walk) |
| 198 | `count_matches` fetches every matching item to answer "is this more than zero" (CLOSED — phase B) | Filed by the prefetch phase's wrap, and the reason it is a row is that **it has been deferred repeatedly and had nowhere to be deferred TO**. Originally 9c Task 2 Minor 7. What it is: `collections/smart.py:106-127`'s `count_matches` calls `section.fetchItems(…)` and returns `len(items)`, so validating a smart filter that matches four thousand items pulls four thousand items across the wire to learn that the answer is "more than none". Its only caller that needs the number as a *decision* is `require_matches` (`:130-148`), which refuses at zero and otherwise uses the count for one action string; the dynamic engine's `minimum_items` is the other caller and genuinely wants the number. **Two things keep it a Minor rather than a defect, and both were re-verified against shipped code when this row was filed rather than carried forward as claims.** It matches `plex_search.build`'s own precedent — `builders/plex_search.py:354` performs the identical unbounded `fetchItems` on the same URL shape — though the precedent is imperfect in this row's favour: `plex_search` NEEDS those items, because they are the collection's membership, and this call needs only their count. And it fires only on definition CHANGE: `smart.py:307` guards it with `if not definition_current`, so a pass over unchanged smart definitions pays nothing at all, and the cost lands on the pass after an operator edits a filter. **The deferral history, stated rather than implied.** What is on disk is one DEFER-with-destination in the 9c branch review — "follow-up row beside the metadata-prefetch/budget family (155/180); it matches `plex_search.build`'s own precedent and fires only on definition change" (`.superpowers/sdd/branch-review-9c.md:144`) — and one ledger line (`.superpowers/sdd/progress.md:2714`). No row in this table, through 9c, 10a-1, 10a-2, 10b, 10c-lite and Phase A's own planning; the phase-A adjudication that ordered it filed counts this as its **fifth** deferral. The destination it was given was "beside the metadata-prefetch/budget family", that family was Phase A, and this is Phase A's wrap — so it is filed here rather than deferred a sixth time, which is the whole point. **What closing it would take:** a count that does not materialise the items. So `count_matches` is now one tiny GET through `section._server.query(…, headers=…)` reading `totalSize`, and it never materialises the items — pinned by a test whose fake section's `fetchItems` is poisoned, so "does not fetch" is an assertion rather than a claim. A response with NO `totalSize` is a failure and not a zero, because reading one as the other would refuse a perfectly good filter at `require_matches`; zero MATCHES is an answer, an unreadable count is not. **The fifth deferral is DISCHARGED with it.** 9c's carry (`branch-review-9c.md:144`) was that `require_matches`' unbounded fetch had nowhere to be deferred to; it inherits the cheap read through this one function, as does the dynamic engine's `minimum_items` — no caller had to decide whether to share it, because the sharing was already the shape. `section._server` joins `_uriRoot` and the session in `tests/test_plexapi_collection_contract.py`, pinned along with `query`'s `headers=` parameter | S — one probe to confirm the container-size read, then one function; `minimum_items`' caller decides whether it shares it | parity-only (a cost, not a behaviour; the count it returns is correct today) | 197 (its filed neighbourhood), 102 |
| 199 | The franchise pack builds a family Kometa would not: no membership floor, and 29 collections short of upstream's members (CLOSED — the location-names phase, by decision) | Filed by the prefetch phase's wrap on row 190's precedent — "a divergence that lives only in a module docstring is one nobody finds" — and on its Task 4 review's ruling, which named two divergences of one class and said one row covers both. `content_franchises` ships READY on the `facts_family` / `tmdb_collection` enumeration row 192 closed, and it is a faithful transcription of `defaults/movie/franchise.yml`'s consumable tables: twelve `addons` pairs and three `title_override` entries, byte-for-byte and in file order, plus upstream's own `remove_suffix: Collection` and `custom_keys: false`. **Three of upstream's keys are not ported, and two of the three change what an operator's collections CONTAIN.** **(1) `minimum_items: 2`.** Upstream builds no collection for a franchise the library holds one film of; this service builds one. The knob is not merely unimplemented — `FactsFamilyParams`' docstring refuses it and prices the refusal: a unit's membership is resolved by the unit's OWN builder one layer down, so a per-key minimum here would mean resolving every key's membership twice, and for `tmdb_collection` that second pass is a TMDb read per franchise taken *before* deciding whether the franchise is worth building. A network cost, not a filter. The divergence is operator-visible on any library with many singletons, and it **interacts with the pack's `max_collections: 250`** — singleton franchises consume that budget, and the budget's failure mode is a loud refusal that creates nothing, so enough singletons can stop the family building at all. **That refusal is not free.** The franchise family names every enumerated key *before* the cap is checked (`builders/facts_family.py:288-311`, then `:352`) — one `/collection/{id}` read each, provider-cached per TTL — so a library over the cap re-pays the whole naming cost on every pass the cache has expired, refusing loudly and correctly each time. An early bound is awkward rather than impossible: `addons:` can merge keys down below the cap, so the only sound early refusal is on `len(counted) - merged_addon_keys > max_collections`, which is the fix if anyone ever wants it. That cap is itself the pack's one unevidenced number, disclosed as a judgement at its site and anchored at five times the builder's default of 50, because the value set is this service's own `item_facts` and no probe has ever counted franchises on a real library. **(2) `template_variables.movie` — 29 keys, 44 members.** Upstream merges loose movie ids into 29 of its franchise collections (Hobbs & Shaw into The Fast and the Furious, Once Upon a Deadpool into X-Men); this service does not, so those 29 collections are missing films Kometa puts in them. Not portable at the params layer: membership here is the franchise's own `parts` through a builder that takes one collection id, so there is no seam a loose id could be added at. **(3) `name_mapping`** is upstream's asset-FOLDER name and has no meaning in a service that resolves posters through its own providers — listed for completeness; it changes nothing an operator sees. **A fourth divergence is structural rather than an omission**, and is already stated in the preset's own description: an `addons` bucket merging two franchises builds the FIRST and names the rest in the pass report, because the shipped `tmdb_collection` builder takes a single id. Twelve buckets can reach that shape, and only on a library holding both halves. **One further edge belongs beside these rather than under them**, because it is a failure mode rather than a divergence: a TMDb 404 on `/collection/{id}` leaves a franchise unnameable, so its key derives no title and is absent from an otherwise-successful family's `generated` set — the one PER-KEY erosion path the family-level sweep protection does not cover, since that protection is all-or-nothing per family. | 204 | `filters:` normalises no values while `plex_search` resolves them, so the same attribute name takes two value vocabularies | Filed by phase B's wrap out of the Task 3 review's controller adjudication, as row 190's precedent demands: a divergence that lives only in a module docstring is one nobody finds. **The mechanism.** The batched accessor hands back whatever the enrichment carries, and for the language rows that is Plex's `languageTag` — ISO 639-1, `en`. The asymmetry is a consequence of two individually-correct choices — the batched read stores codes, and the accessor performs no request — and the second is the load-bearing one: resolving `English` → `en` the way `plex_search` does means asking the server for its choice list, which is the ONE thing the tier-1 accessor layer may never do (`filter_values.py:8`). **Current cover:** the two language rows in `collections/filters.py` state that the values are ISO 639-1 codes and tell the operator to write `audio_language: en`. **What closing it would take:** a value-normalisation seam on the `filters:` side that is offline by construction — a `langcodes`-backed base-code fold would answer `eng` and `pt-BR` without a request; `English` needs a name table this tree does not have, which is the same table row 190's language TITLES lack and row 196's country NAMES lack, so the three are neighbours — that table exists now (`collections/iso_names.py`), so the residue is one seam, not a table. **The code-spelling half SHIPPED with the location-names phase:** `filters:` now compares the two language attributes at the BASE code — `base_language_code` moved from `builders/plex_search.py` into `collections/filters.py`, because the model layer must not import a builder, and both sides of the tag comparison fold through it — so `audio_language: eng` answers, and a written base code meets a regional stream tag (`pt` against `pt-BR`), offline by construction, zero Plex reads. One judgement rides with it, disclosed at the site and pinned by test: a regional WRITTEN value (`es-419`) matches at its base here, where `plex_search` targets an exact library value only. That is a deliberate widening on this side, not an oversight, and it is the one place the two vocabularies still differ in the other direction. **The display-title half stays OPEN in this row** — `audio_language: English` still matches nothing — but what it waits on has changed: no longer a table this tree lacks, only a seam that consults the one it now has, `collections/iso_names.py`'s `LANGUAGE_NAMES`, vendored by row 196's fetch | S — the offline code fold SHIPPED; the display-title residue is one seam over an existing table | parity-only (the block loads and answers; only the spelling an operator guesses decides whether it answers right) | 155 (closed — the widening), 190 (closed), 196 (closed — the fetch that supplied the table), 96 |
| 205 | `ElementTree.ParseError` escapes the narrowed exception classes at both stream-read sites (CLOSED — hardening sweep 1) | Filed by the location-names wrap from branch-review-phaseb D1, which was ruled and never filed; the T2 review's item 9 raised the same escape independently, so this is one finding sighted twice rather than a new one. **The mechanism, read out of the installed dependency rather than assumed.** plexapi's `utils.parseXMLString` (`plexapi/utils.py:836-844`, v4.18.2) catches `ParseError` only to RETRY `ElementTree.fromstring` on a cleaned string, and that retry is UNGUARDED — a body that is not XML at all fails the clean-and-retry the same way it failed the first parse, and the second `ParseError` propagates. `ElementTree.ParseError` is a `SyntaxError` subclass (`ParseError → SyntaxError → Exception`), so it is in NEITHER caught class at `collections/enrichment.py:73` nor at `builders/plex_search.py:472` — both catch `(PlexApiException, requests.RequestException)`. The realistic trigger is a reverse proxy or captive portal answering **200 with an HTML error body**: the transport succeeded, so `requests` raises nothing, and plexapi never gets far enough to raise a `PlexApiException`. **The comment at the second site is actively misleading and should be corrected in the same edit:** `plex_search.py:462-464` says "a malformed response or an auth failure is `PlexApiException` itself", which is true for the auth half and false for the malformed half — malformed is exactly the case that escapes. **Why this is hardening and not a correctness bug.** Every shipped consumer contains the escape in a blanket engine/job catch, so the failure becomes a refusal either way and no missing-data-becomes-wrong-write path exists; what is lost is the NON-MEMOISATION (the pass re-attempts a read that will fail again, where a caught failure is cached for the pass) and the log copy — the operator gets a raw traceback instead of the named refusal both sites were written to produce. **The fix adds `ElementTree.ParseError` to the tuple at BOTH sites TOGETHER**, with a red test per site. The class lists are deliberately coupled — enrichment's catch was written from plex_search's and its comment cites that file by line — so correcting one alone breaks the coupling and leaves the next reader with two near-identical tuples that disagree for no recorded reason. Nothing was fixed by the filing phase. **closed hardening-sweep-1:** `ElementTree.ParseError` added to the tuple at BOTH sites in one commit — `collections/enrichment.py:82` and `builders/plex_search.py:479`, identical and in the same order — with the stale malformed-response clause corrected in the same edit (and two further stale phrasings downstream of it, which the brief had not named), plus a red test per site: each showed a raw `xml.etree.ElementTree.ParseError` propagating where the named refusal belonged. **On the report's absolute that "no test can catch drift":** half of it was wrong and that half is now pinned. `0561412` `ast`-parses both modules, identifies each site by a semantic property (the tuple-typed handler that names `PlexApiException` itself, which excludes `plex_search`'s `NotFound`/`BadRequest` handler without depending on ordering) and asserts the two class-name sets are equal — so **drift between the two known sites is pinned**, and its mutation names the offending file. The other half stands: a THIRD stream-read site added later is a review-time property that neither the ast test nor a shared constant can see, and the mechanisms that could are the over-engineering C2 warns off. A shared `_STREAM_READ_FAILURES` constant was considered and rejected on those C2 grounds — it would buy an import edge from a builder into `enrichment.py` for something the ast test gets free. | S — one exception class at two named sites, one stale comment clause, plus a red test per site | hardening (a refusal arrives as a raw traceback rather than a named refusal, and is re-attempted rather than memoised) | phase-B branch review §2/§7 (D1, ruled and never filed), location-names T2 review item 9 |
| 206 | The Phase-A facts columns converge over weeks; nothing offers a one-time catch-up sweep (CLOSED — sweep 2) | Filed by the location-names wrap as the controller's declared default, because the phase shipped two packs whose membership depends on a column that is empty today and fills slowly. **The measurement, not an estimate.** Production's `tmdb_origin_country` is `[]` on all 13590 `item_facts` rows and `tmdb_original_language` is NULL on all of them, re-checked twice against the live database (`docs/research/tmdb-iso-names/README.md` §2, captures at 2026-08-30 06:59Z and 08:56Z, byte-identical). The cause is measured and benign: the ratings-drift sweep is the only thing that revisits a fact row, every row was written in the 08-23…08-28 backfill window, and the sweep only picks up rows older than `drift_max_age_days` — so at the re-check **0 of 2252** candidates were eligible, with the first eligible at `2026-08-30 21:50Z`. The writer is provably healthy (12458 of 13590 rows carry a `tmdb`-sourced field); the columns are never-written, not written-empty. **The pace.** The sweep's population is `kind IN ('movie','show')` = **2252 items**, counted rather than inferred, and it enqueues at most `scheduler.drift_batch_size` = 500 per `scheduler.drift_days` = 7 tick, so a full revisit is `ceil(2252/500)` = **5 ticks ≈ 5 weeks** at the defaults. An operator who enables `location_region`, `location_continent` or the `original_language` family on a settled library therefore watches real-but-small collections grow for about a month, which the three pack descriptions disclose. **The workaround that exists today**, and it is why this is a convenience row rather than a defect: the two knobs above are live config — raising `drift_batch_size` or lowering `drift_days` compresses the same five ticks into days, and lowering `drift_max_age_days` makes rows eligible sooner. Nothing is broken; the default cadence is simply tuned for ratings drift, which is not urgent, and is now also carrying a one-time schema fill, which is. **What closing it takes:** a one-shot catch-up job that walks the parent population once, ignoring the age threshold, rate-limited against TMDb and resumable, triggered on demand rather than scheduled — the drift sweep's own `sweep_stale_facts` with the age predicate dropped and a cursor, not a new pipeline. The open design question is what triggers it: a migration hook fires it for everyone including operators who do not want it, so a UI button or a CLI entry point is the likelier shape. **closed sweep-2:** the row's own design shipped — `backfill_facts` (`scheduler/jobs.py`) is `sweep_stale_facts`' population, valve, stamp and enqueue with the age predicate dropped and a `facts_backfill_state` cursor (walking `MediaItem.id`) in its place; the trigger adjudication landed as the ops-precedent `POST /api/facts/backfill` + a `GET` for standing progress + one button on the Collections page (`FactsBackfillPanel`), NO migration hook per this row's own rejection. Each press enqueues one `drift_batch_size` batch through the normal pipeline; a re-press resumes from the cursor; a drained population answers complete idempotently; an open `TmdbRateBudget` window PARKS the trigger, cursor unmoved, because a batch gathered with TMDb skipped would stamp `fetched_at` and silently lose the columns this exists to fill. Progress is always the live parent count, never this row's measured 2252 | S–M — one job reusing the sweep's query without its age predicate, a cursor, and a trigger | convenience (the facts converge either way; this decides whether it takes days or weeks) | 196 (the packs that wait on the fill), 190, 192 |
| 207 | Credential tracebacks still reach stdout, and `/api/logs` still shows the operator's base URL (CLOSED — sweep 3) | Filed by hardening sweep 1's wrap out of Task 1's review (Minor 1), because row 117 closed on the SERVED surface only and the remainder deserves a row rather than a memory. **What row 117 fixed:** `LogBuffer.emit` scrubs `...api_key`/`apikey`/`token=` query params out of every formatted record before it reaches `/api/logs` or the SSE stream. **What it deliberately did not, and why that is a row.** (a) **stdout.** `main.py:74`'s `logging.basicConfig` installs a root handler that formats independently of the `LogBuffer` handler `app.py:158` adds, so `providers/ladder.py:81` and `candidates.py`'s two fan-out sites still print the full `exc_info` traceback — Fanart's `api_key` included — to the pod log. The repo already holds itself to a stricter rule than that: `main.py:75-81` mutes httpx to WARNING with the words "Neither may reach the pod logs", and the sweep's own constraint reads "tokens/keys never printed". (b) **the operator's base URL.** Row 117's pattern redacts the credential PARAM and leaves the host, so `/api/logs` still serves `http://plex.internal:32400/library/sections/9/all` — which the same constraint's "operator URLs never in logs" clause touches. **Why it is not a one-line fix.** The unscrubbed stdout copy is not an oversight; it is the compensating control rows 136/188 lean on, pinned by `tests/test_scheduler_collections_job.py:285-286` (the token and the cause are both asserted present in `caplog.text`). Any fix must not blind that, so the real question is whether per-sink formatters (scrub-everything for the buffer, full detail for stdout) are the answer, or whether the pod log is accepted as a trusted sink and the rule reworded. Two smaller notes from the same review belong here: the pattern matches `api_key`/`apikey` but not `api-key` (no shipped provider spells it that way, so not live — `api[-_]?key` would close it), and `_scrub` now runs on every record through the process-global root handler, so a pathological long word-character run is rescanned from every offset. **A third note, from the hardening-sweep-1 polish pass:** a percent-encoded `api_key%3D…` — a URL nested inside another URL's query value, so the literal `=` the pattern requires is itself encoded — would pass the scrub unredacted; no shipped provider produces that shape today, so it is not live, but it belongs beside the `api-key` note above if this row is ever worked. Nothing was changed by the filing. **closed sweep-3, on the sink decision:** the pod log is the TRUSTED sink — full detail belongs to stdout (host-only, kubectl-gated), and every served surface carries redacted text; per-sink formatters are NOT built (the one-seam `LogBuffer` scrub is the precedent; a formatter matrix is the YAGNI the standing laws forbid). Under that decision this row's **(a) half is ACCEPTED, plainly: the `exc_info` tracebacks that reach the pod log CARRYING KEYS stay whole** — that unscrubbed stdout copy is the compensating control rows 136/188 lean on, caplog-pinned, and the sweep constraint's "never printed"/"never in logs" wording is hereby read as governing the SERVED surfaces. What closed is the (b) half plus the pattern notes: `_scrub` now also redacts the authority of every URL-shaped host at the same `LogBuffer` seam (`http://REDACTED/library/...`, the path deliberately kept — it is what makes a line debuggable, and the clause is about hosts; public API hosts over-match on purpose, row 117's tradeoff, so `/api/logs` alone no longer tells an operator WHICH provider a bare `https://REDACTED/3/movie/603` came from), `api[-_]?key` closes the api-key spelling note, and `(=\|%3D)` closes the percent-encoded evasion note. The rescan-performance note stays accepted as filed — the added alternations do not change the every-record posture. **A SECOND residual is accepted with the first, and named as plainly: the percent-encoding defence is one level deep, and it covers the CREDENTIAL half only.** Two executed shapes: a double-encoded `?src=…%253Fapi_key%253DSECRETKEY` serves the secret intact (`%25` matches neither `=` nor `%3D`), and — the asymmetry that matters — a nested `?src=http%3A%2F%2Fplex.internal%3A32400%2Fx` carries the OPERATOR HOST through unredacted, because the host clause is anchored on a literal `://` that an encoded URL does not contain. So the two halves of this row now differ on exactly the shape the new `%3D` test dramatises: the nested credential is caught, the nested host is not. | 208 | Suite discipline: no test may order, or narrowly bound, two wall-clock readings (CLOSED — the rule is a guard) | Filed by hardening sweep 1's wrap as the durable half of row 195's investigation. The other half — "the developer's VM has a broken clock" — is not durable work product: it is not a repo change, CI cannot verify it, it has no closing condition, and it rots the moment the machine is reimaged. **The enforceable rule, which is this row:** no test in this suite may assert an ORDERING, or a tolerance under about three seconds, between two wall-clock readings taken at different instants — database clock or host clock alike. It is checkable at review time, it survives a machine change, and it has a measured justification. **The evidence.** `docs/research/dev-clock-step/README.md` carries the measurement (a 120 s probe: 11 646 samples, 4 step events, Postgres `clock_timestamp()` and the container's `time.time()` retreating by the same amount at the same sample, agreeing to under 100 µs), the derivation (a composite **-2.705 s** step every **~30 s** of REAL time; the 27.28 s figure the probe prints is apparent time, short by exactly one step per cycle), the reproducer inline so it cannot evaporate with the gitignored path it was run from, and the immune shapes — `time.perf_counter()`, and any comparison made within a single transaction, where `transaction_timestamp()` is fixed at transaction start and cannot move underneath the reader. Exposure is `interval / 30 s`, where `interval` is the gap between the two readings; the step magnitude only supplies the CONDITION (it inverts any ordering whose interval is under 2.7 s). **The instances, all found and all handled during the sweep:** six assertions across four files — `tests/test_pipeline.py`'s row-195 assertion (~1.12 s interval, ~3.7 %), `tests/test_facts_gather.py:301` and `:340` (~0.1 s, ~0.3 % each), and `tests/test_item_facts.py:69` plus `tests/test_queue.py:119`, whose 1 s tolerances were smaller than the step. The first three were relaxed to re-stamp equality; the last two were RESTRUCTURED onto same-transaction identity rather than widened, which made them strictly stronger — their mutations land 3.5 ms and 374 µs off, so the tolerance they replaced would have passed both defects. **Five of the six are now immune. The sixth is an irreducible residual, and that is the interesting part of this row.** `tests/test_queue.py:309` compares a `run_after` stamped inside `reclaim_stale()`'s transaction against a later `now()`, and `reclaim_stale()` COMMITS — so the comparison is inherently cross-transaction and no test-level restructure removes it. Computing it server-side moves where the comparison happens, not which two readings it compares. It matters beyond the test: `_CLAIM_SQL` matches on `run_after <= now()` and `_RECLAIM_SQL` on `claimed_at < now() - make_interval(...)`, both against rows stamped in earlier committed transactions, so a backwards step in that window mis-schedules real work — a job not yet due, a claim not yet stale. At an order-of-magnitude estimate of ~2 ms — the one measurement on hand is 5.6 ms (`run_after 11:59:33.478596` vs `db_now 11:59:33.484209`, `run-p-hard1-t4-pre-mut.log:50`) — against a ~30 s cycle that is ≲0.02 % (5.6 ms / 30 s ≈ 0.019 %, not the ~0.007 % the 2 ms estimate implied) and has never been sighted, but it is the APPLICATION's exposure, not the suite's. **So the rule bans avoidable cross-transaction wall-clock assertions; it cannot ban the ones that are the behaviour under test**, and a close that claimed "six of six immune" would have been a wrong record. Two sites were checked and deliberately left alone, recorded so the next sweep does not re-derive it: `tests/test_queue.py:92` and `tests/test_routes.py:131` both compare `run_after` against a LATER `db_now` with seconds of slack, and a backwards-only step makes `db_now` smaller, which only makes them easier to satisfy. **Scope caveat, carried verbatim from the investigation:** this is a developer-environment fact, not a known CI fact — a Linux runner may well be monotonic. The suite is written to survive both, which is why the rule is about the assertions and not about anyone's machine. An operator on this machine can look at Docker Desktop / WSL2 time sync (`wsl --shutdown`), and nothing here depends on their doing so. **No outstanding code:** the six sites are handled and the residual is named; what remains is the rule, applied at review time **CLOSED 2026-09-07, with the rule enforced rather than remembered.** The row's "no outstanding code" was true of the six assertions the sweep audited and false of the suite: the predicate the production queue matches on compares exactly the same two readings, far more often than any assertion does, under ~50 test call sites. Those are fixed (rows 119/193). What closes this row is `tests/test_suite_discipline.py` — an AST walk over `tests/*.py`, non-recursive so the vendored oracle is out of scope — carrying four rules: **(1)** a host-clock read (`datetime.now`, `datetime.utcnow`, `time.time`; never `func.now()`, which Postgres evaluates) inside a comparison; **(2)** a `timedelta` under three seconds — the step is 2.705 s — as an operand of a comparison; **(3)** an **ordering** between two stored clock readings, with a six-entry allowlist, each entry keyed by module and test name rather than by line and each carrying its written reason — `test_api_full_pass.py` and `test_run_history.py` are immune by construction (one transaction, one `transaction_timestamp()`), and `test_queue.py`'s two backoff assertions, its wake assertion and `test_routes.py`'s settle-window assertion are safe by direction, since a backwards-only step shrinks `db_now` and only makes them easier; **(4)** `run_after` stamped from a bare `now()` read in a test, which is the rule that keeps the fix from being undone. Equalities are deliberately not flagged under rule 3: same-transaction identity is what the sweep converted the flaky orderings INTO, so flagging it would flag the fix. **What the guard cannot catch, recorded rather than glossed:** the claim-predicate family itself, because there is no comparison in the test at all — the comparison is inside the SQL; nor subscripted operands (`test_queue.py:566`); nor server-side comparisons built with `func` inside a `select` (`test_queue.py:508`, `test_scheduler_stale_reclaim_job.py:67`), both of which are the deliberate irreducible idiom. That is why the guard ships **with** the fix and not instead of it. Four negative-case tests prove each rule fires, and a freshness test fails on an allowlist entry whose site no longer exists. No lane edit: `pyproject.toml:81` sets `testpaths = ["tests"]` with pytest's default `test_*.py` collection, and `tests/test_ci_path_filters.py`'s gate covers `test_api_*.py` only. | S — the code is done; this row is the rule plus its evidence | parity-only (test infrastructure) | 195 |

| 212 | The `/api/logs` scrub's percent-encoding defence is one level deep, and covers the credential half only (CLOSED — sweep 5) | Filed by sweep 3's wrap out of Task 1's review (Minor 1), because row 207 closed having ACCEPTED this residual and an accepted-but-unfiled gap is one nobody finds. **The mechanism.** Both patterns in `api/logs.py` are anchored on literal characters an encoded URL does not contain. `_CREDENTIAL_PARAM` matches `=` or `%3D` — one level of encoding — so a double-encoded `%253D` passes it; `_URL_HOST` is anchored on a literal `://`, so a URL nested inside another URL's query value (`?src=http%3A%2F%2Fplex.internal%3A32400%2Fx`) keeps its authority on the served surface. **The asymmetry is why this is a row rather than a note:** the nested-URL shape is exactly what row 207's new `%3D` test dramatises, and after that fix the nested CREDENTIAL is redacted while the nested HOST beside it is not. **Not live today** — no shipped provider or client produces a doubly-nested URL in a logged message; the shapes above were found by adversarial probing of the compiled patterns, not in a captured log line, which is why they were accepted rather than fixed mid-sweep. **What closing it would take, and why it wants an adjudication rather than an executor's judgement:** either unquote the text before scrubbing (catches every depth in one move, but changes what the served line LOOKS like — an operator would read a decoded URL where the logging site wrote an encoded one, and the scrub would stop being a pure redaction) or add an encoded-scheme alternation to the host pattern (`https?(://\|%3A%2F%2F)` — leaves the text alone, but buys exactly one more level, and the same question returns at `%25`). The pod log keeps the full line under row 207's trusted-sink decision either way, so nothing here is urgent. **closed sweep-5:** by the alternation, not the unquote — decode-before-scrub changes what the served line looks like and rewrites literal %XX in arbitrary log text, breaking the doctrine's "same text, less of it" shape, so the adjudication took the same move row 207 made for the credential half, one level up: the host scrub gains a second, encoded-scheme pattern beside the untouched `_URL_HOST` (`_URL_HOST_ENCODED`, matching `https?%3A%2F%2F`), and the encoded-authority match terminates at `%2F` so the nested URL's encoded path survives exactly as the plain path does (the path-survives pin extends to the encoded form). Seen RED on the served surface — `plex.internal` served whole inside the nested shape — before landing green (`run-p-sweep5-t1-red.log`). The tempering is scoped to the ENCODED branch as a SEPARATE pattern (`_URL_HOST_ENCODED`), because sharing one character class between both branches — as the fix first shipped — also stopped the PLAIN branch at any literal `%2F` in the authority (percent-encoded userinfo), serving a host that `c79d7f4` redacted whole; that regression was caught in review and fixed by splitting the patterns, with a 200 000-case differential fuzz confirming the plain form is exactly as untempered as it was at `c79d7f4`. **The residuals, accepted and named on the row's own terms.** (1) **Double-encoding.** `%253A` passes — the alternation buys exactly one level and the same question returns at the next — no shipped client produces it, and the pod log keeps the full line under the trusted-sink decision. (2) **The quoted-host fail-open**, new with the row-214 half and documented in the pattern's own comment: `_HOST_KEYWORD`'s value class is tempered against comma, space and the closing quote, so a quoted value CONTAINING a comma or space, or an unterminated quote, makes the whole `host=` match fail and the value is served INTACT — a fail-open, not a truncation. Adjudicated acceptable because `requests` never writes a hostname shaped that way, so no live carrier is affected. (3) **The quote-safe semi-encoded shape.** `urllib.parse.quote`'s default `safe='/'` leaves the slashes literal and encodes only the colon, so quoting a URL yields `http%3A//host%3A32400/x` — matched by NEITHER `_URL_HOST` (needs a literal `://`) NOR `_URL_HOST_ENCODED` (needs `%3A%2F%2F`). Probed, not sighted: no call to `quote(` exists anywhere in `src/`, and the pod log keeps the full line either way | S — one pattern alternation or one unquote seam, plus the choice between them | none (a residual leak on the served surface, not a feature) | 207 (closed — the row whose close accepted this residual), 117 |
